# GOGI Official Teachable Corpus — Claude Packet

Purpose: This packet shows the official Grade 9 text map as GOGI currently understands it, with each classroom-ready excerpt tied to the exact standard and subskill it can teach.

Use rules:
- Use only the excerpts and questions shown here unless Carlos explicitly asks you to generate new material.
- Treat “Gold card” entries as classroom-ready seeds for lessons, workbook pages, and FAST-style practice.
- If a text has no cards or a standard gap, say the corpus needs more curation instead of pretending it is covered.
- Preserve the standard/subskill lane when building lessons: do not use a setting card as a POV card, a theme card as a rhetoric card, etc.

## The Talented Tenth — W.E.B. Du Bois
- Source status: stored
- Local source available: yes
- Word count: 7,318
- Official mapped standards: ELA.9.R.2.1, ELA.9.R.2.2, ELA.9.R.2.4, ELA.9.R.3.2

### ELA.9.R.2.1 — Text structure and feature purpose

#### Card 1: Cause and effect structure
- Location: paragraphs 13-14
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> The most interesting question, and in many respects the crucial question, to be asked concerning college-bred Negroes, is: Do they earn a living? It has been intimated more than once that the higher training of Negroes has resulted in sending into the world of work, men who could find nothing to do suitable to their talents. Now and then there comes a rumor of a colored college man working at menial service, etc. Fortunately, returns as to occupations of college-bred Negroes, gathered by the Atlanta conference, are quite full--nearly sixty per cent. of the total number of graduates.
> These figures illustrate vividly the function of the college-bred Negro. He is, as he ought to be, the group leader, the man who sets the ideals of the community where he lives, directs its thoughts and heads its social movements. It need hardly be argued that the Negro people need social leadership more than most groups; that they have no traditions to fall back upon, no long established customs, no strong family ties, no well defined social classes. All these things must be slowly and painfully evolved. The preacher was, even before the war, the group leader of the Negroes, and the church their greatest social institution. Naturally this preacher was ignorant and often immoral, and the problem of replacing the older type by better educated men has been a difficult one. Both by direct work and by direct influence on other preachers, and on congregations, the college-bred preacher has an opportunity for reformatory work and moral inspiration, the value of which cannot be overestimated.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois use cause and effect to answer criticism of higher education?

A. He avoids the criticism by changing the topic to unrelated schools instead of answering the concern.
B. He argues that education causes students to reject leadership and abandon their communities completely.
**C. He presents a criticism about job outcomes, then uses evidence to show education’s larger social value.**
D. He presents only emotional language without showing any result connected to graduates or work.

Evidence points: has resulted in sending into the world of work, men who could find nothing to do suitable to their talents | It has been intimated more than once that the higher training of Negroes has resulted in sending into the world of work, men who could find nothing to d...

#### Card 2: Cause and effect structure
- Location: paragraphs 21-22
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> The truth of this has been strikingly shown in the marked improvement of white teachers in the South. Twenty years ago the rank and file of white public school teachers were not as good as the Negro teachers. But they, by scholarships and good salaries, have been encouraged to thorough normal and collegiate preparation, while the Negro teachers have been discouraged by starvation wages and the idea that any training will do for a black teacher. If carpenters are needed it is well and good to train men as carpenters. But to train men as carpenters, and then set them to teaching is wasteful and criminal; and to train men as teachers and then refuse them living wages, unless they become carpenters, is rank nonsense.
> And he concludes: "While the number in colored high schools and colleges had increased somewhat faster than the population, it had not kept pace with the average of the whole country, for it had fallen from 30 per cent. to 24 per cent. of the average quota. Of all colored pupils, one (1) in one hundred was engaged in secondary and higher work, and that ratio has continued substantially for the past twenty years. If the ratio of colored population in secondary and higher education is to be equal to the average for the whole country, it must be increased to five times its present average." And if this be true of the secondary and higher education, it is safe to say that the Negro has not one-tenth his quota in college studies. How baseless, therefore, is the charge of too much training! We need Negro teachers for the Negro common schools, and we need first-class normal schools and colleges to train them. This is the work of higher Negro education and it must be done.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois use cause and effect in the carpenters-and-teachers section?

A. He argues that trade training should replace all teacher training because schools no longer matter.
**B. He shows that mismatched training and unfair pay create waste, strengthening his argument for proper education.**
C. He gives a random example that does not connect to education, wages, or public need.
D. He claims that wages have no effect on the quality of teaching or professional preparation.

Evidence points: If carpenters are needed it is well and good to train men as carpenters.

#### Card 3: Chronological or sequence structure
- Location: paragraphs 11-13
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> In addition to these students in the South, Negroes have attended Northern colleges for many years. As early as 1826 one was graduated from Bowdoin College, and from that time till to-day nearly every year has seen elsewhere, other such graduates. They have, of course, met much color prejudice. Fifty years ago very few colleges would admit them at all. Even to-day no Negro has ever been admitted to Princeton, and at some other leading institutions they are rather endured than encouraged. Oberlin was the great pioneer in the work of blotting out the color line in colleges, and has more Negro graduates by far than any other Northern college.
> Of these graduates 2,079 were men and 252 were women; 50 per cent. of Northern-born college men come South to work among the masses of their people, at a sacrifice which few people realize; nearly 90 per cent. of the Southern-born graduates instead of seeking that personal freedom and broader intellectual atmosphere which their training has led them, in some degree, to conceive, stay and labor and wait in the midst of their black neighbors and relatives.
> The most interesting question, and in many respects the crucial question, to be asked concerning college-bred Negroes, is: Do they earn a living? It has been intimated more than once that the higher training of Negroes has resulted in sending into the world of work, men who could find nothing to do suitable to their talents. Now and then there comes a rumor of a colored college man working at menial service, etc. Fortunately, returns as to occupations of college-bred Negroes, gathered by the Atlanta conference, are quite full--nearly sixty per cent. of the total number of graduates.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois use time order to develop his point about college access?

A. He lists colleges only to suggest that college access has always been equal and uncontested.
**B. He traces change over time, showing that Black students gained some access while prejudice and limits still remained.**
C. He tells events out of order so the reader cannot follow the historical change in access.
D. He shifts away from education and focuses only on personal memories that do not support his claim.

Evidence points: and from that time till to-day nearly every year has seen elsewhere, other such graduates. | As early as 1826 one was graduated from Bowdoin College

#### Card 4: Chronological or sequence structure
- Location: paragraphs 2-4
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> If this be true--and who can deny it--three tasks lay before me; first to show from the past that the Talented Tenth as they have risen among American Negroes have been worthy of leadership; secondly, to show how these men may be educated and developed; and thirdly, to show their relation to the Negro problem.
> "Suffer me to recall to your mind that time, in which the arms of the British crown were exerted with every powerful effort, in order to reduce you to a state of servitude; look back, I entreat you, on the variety of dangers to which you were exposed; reflect on that period in which every human aid appeared unavailable, and in which even hope and fortitude wore the aspect of inability to the conflict, and you cannot but be led to a serious and grateful sense of your miraculous and providential preservation, you cannot but acknowledge, that the present freedom and tranquility which you enjoy, you have mercifully received, and that a peculiar blessing of heaven.
> Then came Dr. James Derham, who could tell even the learned Dr. Rush something of medicine, and Lemuel Haynes, to whom Middlebury College gave an honorary A.M. in 1804. These and others we may call the Revolutionary group of distinguished Negroes--they were persons of marked ability, leaders of a Talented Tenth, standing conspicuously among the best of their time. They strove by word and deed to save the color line from becoming the line between the bond and free, but all they could do was nullified by Eli Whitney and the Curse of Gold. So they passed into forgetfulness.

FAST-style question: How does the numbered sequence help Du Bois develop his purpose?

**A. It previews the order of his argument so the reader can follow what he will prove and why it matters.**
B. It mainly entertains the reader by telling a personal story instead of organizing the claims he will prove.
C. It shows that Du Bois has no clear plan for developing his argument or connecting each part.
D. It shifts from argument to description before the reader understands how the major points connect.

Evidence points: and thirdly, to show their relation to the Negro problem. | first to show from the past that the Talented Tenth as they have risen among American Negroes have been worthy of leadership; | secondly, to show how these men may be educated and developed;

#### Card 5: Compare and contrast structure
- Location: paragraphs 7-9
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Can the masses of the Negro people be in any possible way more quickly raised than by the effort and example of this aristocracy of talent and character? Was there ever a nation on God's fair earth civilized from the bottom upward? Never; it is, ever was and ever will be from the top downward that culture filters. The Talented Tenth rises and pulls all that are worth the saving up to their vantage ground. This is the history of human progress; and the two historic mistakes which have hindered that progress were the thinking first that no more could ever rise save the few already risen; or second, that it would better the unrisen to pull the risen down.
> How then shall the leaders of a struggling people be trained and the hands of the risen few strengthened? There can be but one answer: The best and most capable of their youth must be schooled in the colleges and universities of the land. We will not quarrel as to just what the university of the Negro should teach or how it should teach it--I willingly admit that each soul and each race-soul needs its own peculiar curriculum. But this is true: A university is a human invention for the transmission of knowledge and culture from generation to generation, through the training of quick minds and pure hearts, and for this work no other human invention will suffice, not even trade and industrial schools.
> Was the work of these college founders successful; did it stand the test of time? Did the college graduates, with all their fine theories of life, really live? Are they useful men helping to civilize and elevate their less fortunate fellows? Let us see. Omitting all institutions which have not actually graduated students from a college course, there are to-day in the United States thirty-four institutions giving something above high school training to Negroes and designed especially for this race.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois use the contrast between “bottom upward” and “top downward” to develop his argument?

A. He argues that social progress happens without leadership, education, or organized community support.
B. He contrasts two locations but does not connect either image to his argument about progress.
**C. He contrasts two models of progress to argue that trained leaders can help lift the larger community.**
D. He shows that colleges weaken communities by separating students from others permanently.

Evidence points: it is, ever was and ever will be from the top downward that culture filters.

#### Card 6: Example or evidence structure
- Location: paragraphs 12-14
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Of these graduates 2,079 were men and 252 were women; 50 per cent. of Northern-born college men come South to work among the masses of their people, at a sacrifice which few people realize; nearly 90 per cent. of the Southern-born graduates instead of seeking that personal freedom and broader intellectual atmosphere which their training has led them, in some degree, to conceive, stay and labor and wait in the midst of their black neighbors and relatives.
> The most interesting question, and in many respects the crucial question, to be asked concerning college-bred Negroes, is: Do they earn a living? It has been intimated more than once that the higher training of Negroes has resulted in sending into the world of work, men who could find nothing to do suitable to their talents. Now and then there comes a rumor of a colored college man working at menial service, etc. Fortunately, returns as to occupations of college-bred Negroes, gathered by the Atlanta conference, are quite full--nearly sixty per cent. of the total number of graduates.
> These figures illustrate vividly the function of the college-bred Negro. He is, as he ought to be, the group leader, the man who sets the ideals of the community where he lives, directs its thoughts and heads its social movements. It need hardly be argued that the Negro people need social leadership more than most groups; that they have no traditions to fall back upon, no long established customs, no strong family ties, no well defined social classes. All these things must be slowly and painfully evolved. The preacher was, even before the war, the group leader of the Negroes, and the church their greatest social institution. Naturally this preacher was ignorant and often immoral, and the problem of replacing the older type by better educated men has been a difficult one. Both by direct work and by direct influence on other preachers, and on congregations, the college-bred preacher has an opportunity for reformatory work and moral inspiration, the value of which cannot be overestimated.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois use statistics about graduates to develop his point?

A. The statistics show that graduates avoid community work whenever possible after college.
**B. The statistics show that many college-educated Black graduates serve their communities, supporting his claim about leadership.**
C. The statistics prove that higher education has no practical effects on leadership.
D. The statistics shift the argument away from education to entertainment and personal preference.

#### Card 7: Example or evidence structure
- Location: paragraphs 13-14
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> The most interesting question, and in many respects the crucial question, to be asked concerning college-bred Negroes, is: Do they earn a living? It has been intimated more than once that the higher training of Negroes has resulted in sending into the world of work, men who could find nothing to do suitable to their talents. Now and then there comes a rumor of a colored college man working at menial service, etc. Fortunately, returns as to occupations of college-bred Negroes, gathered by the Atlanta conference, are quite full--nearly sixty per cent. of the total number of graduates.
> These figures illustrate vividly the function of the college-bred Negro. He is, as he ought to be, the group leader, the man who sets the ideals of the community where he lives, directs its thoughts and heads its social movements. It need hardly be argued that the Negro people need social leadership more than most groups; that they have no traditions to fall back upon, no long established customs, no strong family ties, no well defined social classes. All these things must be slowly and painfully evolved. The preacher was, even before the war, the group leader of the Negroes, and the church their greatest social institution. Naturally this preacher was ignorant and often immoral, and the problem of replacing the older type by better educated men has been a difficult one. Both by direct work and by direct influence on other preachers, and on congregations, the college-bred preacher has an opportunity for reformatory work and moral inspiration, the value of which cannot be overestimated.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois use evidence about occupations to answer a challenge?

A. He admits that there is no evidence about college graduates’ work or public role.
B. He uses the data to argue that leadership is unnecessary for the community.
**C. He uses gathered data to answer doubts about graduates and show their community leadership.**
D. He changes the topic from education to a personal complaint about employment.

Evidence points: Fortunately, returns as to occupations of college-bred Negroes, gathered by the Atlanta conference, are quite full--nearly sixty per cent. | These figures illustrate vividly the function of the college-bred Negro.

#### Card 8: Example or evidence structure
- Location: paragraphs 14-15
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> These figures illustrate vividly the function of the college-bred Negro. He is, as he ought to be, the group leader, the man who sets the ideals of the community where he lives, directs its thoughts and heads its social movements. It need hardly be argued that the Negro people need social leadership more than most groups; that they have no traditions to fall back upon, no long established customs, no strong family ties, no well defined social classes. All these things must be slowly and painfully evolved. The preacher was, even before the war, the group leader of the Negroes, and the church their greatest social institution. Naturally this preacher was ignorant and often immoral, and the problem of replacing the older type by better educated men has been a difficult one. Both by direct work and by direct influence on other preachers, and on congregations, the college-bred preacher has an opportunity for reformatory work and moral inspiration, the value of which cannot be overestimated.
> It has, however, been in the furnishing of teachers that the Negro college has found its peculiar function. Few persons realize how vast a work, how mighty a revolution has been thus accomplished. To furnish five millions and more of ignorant people with teachers of their own race and blood, in one generation, was not only a very difficult undertaking, but a very important one, in that, it placed before the eyes of almost every Negro child an attainable ideal. It brought the masses of the blacks in contact with modern civilization, made black men the leaders of their communities and trainers of the new generation. In this work college-bred Negroes were first teachers, and then teachers of teachers. And here it is that the broad culture of college work has been of peculiar value. Knowledge of life and its wider meaning, has been the point of the Negro's deepest ignorance, and the sending out of teachers whose training has not been simply for bread winning, but also for human culture, has been of inestimable value in the training of these men.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois use evidence about occupations to answer a challenge?

A. He admits that there is no evidence about college graduates’ work or public role.
B. He uses the data to argue that leadership is unnecessary for the community.
C. He changes the topic from education to a personal complaint about employment.
**D. He uses gathered data to answer doubts about graduates and show their community leadership.**

Evidence points: These figures illustrate vividly the function of the college-bred Negro.

#### Card 9: Example or evidence structure
- Location: paragraphs 9-11
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Was the work of these college founders successful; did it stand the test of time? Did the college graduates, with all their fine theories of life, really live? Are they useful men helping to civilize and elevate their less fortunate fellows? Let us see. Omitting all institutions which have not actually graduated students from a college course, there are to-day in the United States thirty-four institutions giving something above high school training to Negroes and designed especially for this race.
> Three of these were established in border States before the War; thirteen were planted by the Freedmen's Bureau in the years 1864-1869; nine were established between 1870 and 1880 by various church bodies; five were established after 1881 by Negro churches, and four are state institutions supported by United States' agricultural funds. In most cases the college departments are small adjuncts to high and common school work. As a matter of fact six institutions--Atlanta, Fisk, Howard, Shaw, Wilberforce and Leland, are the important Negro colleges so far as actual work and number of students are concerned. In all these institutions, seven hundred and fifty Negro college students are enrolled. In grade the best of these colleges are about a year behind the smaller New England colleges and a typical curriculum is that of Atlanta University. Here students from the grammar grades, after a three years' high school course, take a college course of 136 weeks. One-fourth of this time is given to Latin and Greek; one-fifth, to English and modern languages; one-sixth, to history and social science; one-seventh, to natural science; one-eighth to mathematics, and one-eighth to philosophy and pedagogy.
> In addition to these students in the South, Negroes have attended Northern colleges for many years. As early as 1826 one was graduated from Bowdoin College, and from that time till to-day nearly every year has seen elsewhere, other such graduates. They have, of course, met much color prejudice. Fifty years ago very few colleges would admit them at all. Even to-day no Negro has ever been admitted to Princeton, and at some other leading institutions they are rather endured than encouraged. Oberlin was the great pioneer in the work of blotting out the color line in colleges, and has more Negro graduates by far than any other Northern college.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois use institutional evidence to support his argument?

**A. He gives numbers and school categories to show that higher education has a real history and foundation.**
B. He uses numbers to show that no colleges existed for Black students during this period.
C. He replaces evidence with a fictional story about one student’s private experience.
D. He lists schools only to distract from his argument about education and leadership.

Evidence points: there are to-day in the United States thirty-four institutions giving something above high school training to Negroes and designed especially for this race

#### Card 11: Opening, closing, or shift structure
- Location: paragraphs 2-4
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> If this be true--and who can deny it--three tasks lay before me; first to show from the past that the Talented Tenth as they have risen among American Negroes have been worthy of leadership; secondly, to show how these men may be educated and developed; and thirdly, to show their relation to the Negro problem.
> "Suffer me to recall to your mind that time, in which the arms of the British crown were exerted with every powerful effort, in order to reduce you to a state of servitude; look back, I entreat you, on the variety of dangers to which you were exposed; reflect on that period in which every human aid appeared unavailable, and in which even hope and fortitude wore the aspect of inability to the conflict, and you cannot but be led to a serious and grateful sense of your miraculous and providential preservation, you cannot but acknowledge, that the present freedom and tranquility which you enjoy, you have mercifully received, and that a peculiar blessing of heaven.
> Then came Dr. James Derham, who could tell even the learned Dr. Rush something of medicine, and Lemuel Haynes, to whom Middlebury College gave an honorary A.M. in 1804. These and others we may call the Revolutionary group of distinguished Negroes--they were persons of marked ability, leaders of a Talented Tenth, standing conspicuously among the best of their time. They strove by word and deed to save the color line from becoming the line between the bond and free, but all they could do was nullified by Eli Whitney and the Curse of Gold. So they passed into forgetfulness.

FAST-style question: How does this section help organize Du Bois’s argument?

**A. It announces the argument’s major tasks, helping the reader understand the order and purpose.**
B. It ends the argument before Du Bois gives any evidence or develops his claims.

Evidence points: If this be true--and who can deny it--three tasks lay before me;

#### Card 12: Opening, closing, or shift structure
- Location: paragraphs 8-9
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> How then shall the leaders of a struggling people be trained and the hands of the risen few strengthened? There can be but one answer: The best and most capable of their youth must be schooled in the colleges and universities of the land. We will not quarrel as to just what the university of the Negro should teach or how it should teach it--I willingly admit that each soul and each race-soul needs its own peculiar curriculum. But this is true: A university is a human invention for the transmission of knowledge and culture from generation to generation, through the training of quick minds and pure hearts, and for this work no other human invention will suffice, not even trade and industrial schools.
> Was the work of these college founders successful; did it stand the test of time? Did the college graduates, with all their fine theories of life, really live? Are they useful men helping to civilize and elevate their less fortunate fellows? Let us see. Omitting all institutions which have not actually graduated students from a college course, there are to-day in the United States thirty-four institutions giving something above high school training to Negroes and designed especially for this race.

C. It presents evidence before the reader knows what question is being answered or why.
D. It makes the section mainly descriptive instead of argumentative or purposeful.

Evidence points: How then shall the leaders of a struggling people be trained and the hands of the risen few strengthened? | Let us see.

#### Card 14: Problem and solution structure
- Location: paragraphs 19-21
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> "We are to-day deliberately rearing millions of our citizens in ignorance, and at the same time limiting the rights of citizenship by educational qualifications. This is unjust. Half the black youth of the land have no opportunities open to them for learning to read, write and cipher. In the discussion as to the proper training of Negro children after they leave the public schools, we have forgotten that they are not yet decently provided with public schools.
> "Propositions are beginning to be made in the South to reduce the already meagre school facilities of Negroes. We congratulate the South on resisting, as much as it has, this pressure, and on the many millions it has spent on Negro education. But it is only fair to point out that Negro taxes and the Negroes' share of the income from indirect taxes and endowments have fully repaid this expenditure, so that the Negro public school system has not in all probability cost the white taxpayers a single cent since the war.
> The truth of this has been strikingly shown in the marked improvement of white teachers in the South. Twenty years ago the rank and file of white public school teachers were not as good as the Negro teachers. But they, by scholarships and good salaries, have been encouraged to thorough normal and collegiate preparation, while the Negro teachers have been discouraged by starvation wages and the idea that any training will do for a black teacher. If carpenters are needed it is well and good to train men as carpenters. But to train men as carpenters, and then set them to teaching is wasteful and criminal; and to train men as teachers and then refuse them living wages, unless they become carpenters, is rank nonsense.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois use problem-solution structure to address schooling?

**A. He identifies inadequate public education as the problem, then points toward fairer support and training.**
B. He argues that the current school system already gives equal opportunity to all students.
C. He presents schools as a minor detail unrelated to citizenship or public responsibility.
D. He focuses only on personal success stories without naming a public problem.

> Of these graduates 2,079 were men and 252 were women; 50 per cent. of Northern-born college men come South to work among the masses of their people, at a sacrifice which few people realize; nearly 90 per cent. of the Southern-born graduates instead of seeking that personal freedom and broader intellectual atmosphere which their training has led them, in some degree, to conceive, stay and labor and wait in the midst of their black neighbors and relatives.

All these things must be slowly and painfully evolved. The preacher was, even before the war, the group leader of the Negroes, and the church their greatest social institution. Naturally this preacher was ignorant and often immoral, and the problem of replacing the older type by better educated men has been a difficult one. Both by direct work and by direct influence on other preachers, and on congregations, the college-bred preacher has an opportunity for reformatory work and moral inspiration, the value of which cannot be overestimated.
> It has, however, been in the furnishing of teachers that the Negro college has found its peculiar function. Few persons realize how vast a work, how mighty a revolution has been thus accomplished. To furnish five millions and more of ignorant people with teachers of their own race and blood, in one generation, was not only a very difficult undertaking, but a very important one, in that, it placed before the eyes of almost every Negro child an attainable ideal. It brought the masses of the blacks in contact with modern civilization, made black men the leaders of their communities and trainers of the new generation. In this work college-bred Negroes were first teachers, and then teachers of teachers. And here it is that the broad culture of college work has been of peculiar value. Knowledge of life and its wider meaning, has been the point of the Negro's deepest ignorance, and the sending out of teachers whose training has not been simply for bread winning, but also for human culture, has been of inestimable value in the training of these men.

FAST-style question: How does the paragraph beginning “These figures illustrate” develop the previous evidence?

A. It rejects the earlier data and claims that numbers cannot support an argument.
B. It introduces an unrelated topic that does not connect to education or leadership.
C. It repeats the same question without explaining what the evidence shows.
**D. It explains what the numbers prove, connecting the data to the broader claim about educated leadership.**

Evidence points: It has, however, been in the furnishing of teachers that the Negro college has found its peculiar function. | These figures illustrate vividly the function of the college-bred Negro. | In this work college-bred Negroes were first teachers

#### Card 7: Central idea across paragraphs
- Location: paragraphs 18-20
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> I am an earnest advocate of manual training and trade teaching for black boys, and for white boys, too. I believe that next to the founding of Negro colleges the most valuable addition to Negro education since the war, has been industrial training for black boys. Nevertheless, I insist that the object of all true education is not to make men carpenters, it is to make carpenters men; there are two means of making the carpenter a man, each equally important: the first is to give the group and community in which he works, liberally trained teachers and leaders to teach him and his family what life means; the second is to give him sufficient intelligence and technical skill to make him an efficient workman; the first object demands the Negro college and college-bred men--not a quantity of such colleges, but a few of excellent quality; not too many college-bred men, but enough to leaven the lump, to inspire the masses, to raise the Talented Tenth to leadership; the second object demands a good system of common schools, well-taught, conveniently located and properly equipped.
> "We are to-day deliberately rearing millions of our citizens in ignorance, and at the same time limiting the rights of citizenship by educational qualifications. This is unjust. Half the black youth of the land have no opportunities open to them for learning to read, write and cipher. In the discussion as to the proper training of Negro children after they leave the public schools, we have forgotten that they are not yet decently provided with public schools.
> "Propositions are beginning to be made in the South to reduce the already meagre school facilities of Negroes. We congratulate the South on resisting, as much as it has, this pressure, and on the many millions it has spent on Negro education. But it is only fair to point out that Negro taxes and the Negroes' share of the income from indirect taxes and endowments have fully repaid this expenditure, so that the Negro public school system has not in all probability cost the white taxpayers a single cent since the war.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois’s contrast about “carpenters” develop the central idea?

**A. It clarifies that practical training matters, but true education should develop the whole person.**
B. It argues that practical training should replace every form of broader education.
C. It changes the topic from education to construction without developing the claim.
D. It suggests that colleges should avoid preparing students for work or leadership.

Evidence points: Nevertheless, I insist that the object of all true education is not to make men carpenters, it is to make carpenters men; | the first object demands the Negro college and college-bred men--not a quantity of such colleges

Excerpt:

> If this be true--and who can deny it--three tasks lay before me; first to show from the past that the Talented Tenth as they have risen among American Negroes have been worthy of leadership; secondly, to show how these men may be educated and developed; and thirdly, to show their relation to the Negro problem.
> "Suffer me to recall to your mind that time, in which the arms of the British crown were exerted with every powerful effort, in order to reduce you to a state of servitude; look back, I entreat you, on the variety of dangers to which you were exposed; reflect on that period in which every human aid appeared unavailable, and in which even hope and fortitude wore the aspect of inability to the conflict, and you cannot but be led to a serious and grateful sense of your miraculous and providential preservation, you cannot but acknowledge, that the present freedom and tranquility which you enjoy, you have mercifully received, and that a peculiar blessing of heaven.
> Then came Dr. James Derham, who could tell even the learned Dr. Rush something of medicine, and Lemuel Haynes, to whom Middlebury College gave an honorary A.M. in 1804. These and others we may call the Revolutionary group of distinguished Negroes--they were persons of marked ability, leaders of a Talented Tenth, standing conspicuously among the best of their time. They strove by word and deed to save the color line from becoming the line between the bond and free, but all they could do was nullified by Eli Whitney and the Curse of Gold. So they passed into forgetfulness.

FAST-style question: How do the listed “three tasks” develop Du Bois’s central idea?

#### Card 9: Central idea across paragraphs
- Location: paragraphs 8-9
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> How then shall the leaders of a struggling people be trained and the hands of the risen few strengthened? There can be but one answer: The best and most capable of their youth must be schooled in the colleges and universities of the land. We will not quarrel as to just what the university of the Negro should teach or how it should teach it--I willingly admit that each soul and each race-soul needs its own peculiar curriculum. But this is true: A university is a human invention for the transmission of knowledge and culture from generation to generation, through the training of quick minds and pure hearts, and for this work no other human invention will suffice, not even trade and industrial schools.
> Was the work of these college founders successful; did it stand the test of time? Did the college graduates, with all their fine theories of life, really live? Are they useful men helping to civilize and elevate their less fortunate fellows? Let us see. Omitting all institutions which have not actually graduated students from a college course, there are to-day in the United States thirty-four institutions giving something above high school training to Negroes and designed especially for this race.

#### Card 11: Central idea stated or implied
- Location: paragraphs 21-22
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> The truth of this has been strikingly shown in the marked improvement of white teachers in the South. Twenty years ago the rank and file of white public school teachers were not as good as the Negro teachers. But they, by scholarships and good salaries, have been encouraged to thorough normal and collegiate preparation, while the Negro teachers have been discouraged by starvation wages and the idea that any training will do for a black teacher. If carpenters are needed it is well and good to train men as carpenters. But to train men as carpenters, and then set them to teaching is wasteful and criminal; and to train men as teachers and then refuse them living wages, unless they become carpenters, is rank nonsense.
> And he concludes: "While the number in colored high schools and colleges had increased somewhat faster than the population, it had not kept pace with the average of the whole country, for it had fallen from 30 per cent. to 24 per cent. of the average quota. Of all colored pupils, one (1) in one hundred was engaged in secondary and higher work, and that ratio has continued substantially for the past twenty years. If the ratio of colored population in secondary and higher education is to be equal to the average for the whole country, it must be increased to five times its present average." And if this be true of the secondary and higher education, it is safe to say that the Negro has not one-tenth his quota in college studies. How baseless, therefore, is the charge of too much training! We need Negro teachers for the Negro common schools, and we need first-class normal schools and colleges to train them. This is the work of higher Negro education and it must be done.

FAST-style question: Which central idea is developed by the example of improved teachers?

A. Teacher quality is unrelated to the education available to a community.
B. The passage argues that schools should avoid measuring teacher improvement.
**C. Educational opportunity can improve teaching quality and strengthen the community it serves.**
D. The example mainly shows that education has no practical effect outside college.

Evidence points: The truth of this has been strikingly shown in the marked improvement of white teachers in the South. | while the Negro teachers have been discouraged by starvation wages and the idea that any training will do for a black teacher.

#### Card 12: Detail versus central idea
- Location: paragraphs 12-14
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Of these graduates 2,079 were men and 252 were women; 50 per cent. of Northern-born college men come South to work among the masses of their people, at a sacrifice which few people realize; nearly 90 per cent. of the Southern-born graduates instead of seeking that personal freedom and broader intellectual atmosphere which their training has led them, in some degree, to conceive, stay and labor and wait in the midst of their black neighbors and relatives.
> The most interesting question, and in many respects the crucial question, to be asked concerning college-bred Negroes, is: Do they earn a living? It has been intimated more than once that the higher training of Negroes has resulted in sending into the world of work, men who could find nothing to do suitable to their talents. Now and then there comes a rumor of a colored college man working at menial service, etc. Fortunately, returns as to occupations of college-bred Negroes, gathered by the Atlanta conference, are quite full--nearly sixty per cent. of the total number of graduates.
> These figures illustrate vividly the function of the college-bred Negro. He is, as he ought to be, the group leader, the man who sets the ideals of the community where he lives, directs its thoughts and heads its social movements. All these things must be slowly and painfully evolved. The preacher was, even before the war, the group leader of the Negroes, and the church their greatest social institution. Naturally this preacher was ignorant and often immoral, and the problem of replacing the older type by better educated men has been a difficult one. Both by direct work and by direct influence on other preachers, and on congregations, the college-bred preacher has an opportunity for reformatory work and moral inspiration, the value of which cannot be overestimated.

D. A detail should be rejected only when it is false, never when it is true but too narrow. The development of the passage points elsewhere. This misses the evidence in the excerpt.

#### Card 16: Evidence develops central idea
- Location: paragraphs 9-11
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Was the work of these college founders successful; did it stand the test of time? Did the college graduates, with all their fine theories of life, really live? Are they useful men helping to civilize and elevate their less fortunate fellows? Let us see. Omitting all institutions which have not actually graduated students from a college course, there are to-day in the United States thirty-four institutions giving something above high school training to Negroes and designed especially for this race.
> Three of these were established in border States before the War; thirteen were planted by the Freedmen's Bureau in the years 1864-1869; nine were established between 1870 and 1880 by various church bodies; five were established after 1881 by Negro churches, and four are state institutions supported by United States' agricultural funds. In most cases the college departments are small adjuncts to high and common school work. As a matter of fact six institutions--Atlanta, Fisk, Howard, Shaw, Wilberforce and Leland, are the important Negro colleges so far as actual work and number of students are concerned. In all these institutions, seven hundred and fifty Negro college students are enrolled. In grade the best of these colleges are about a year behind the smaller New England colleges and a typical curriculum is that of Atlanta University. Here students from the grammar grades, after a three years' high school course, take a college course of 136 weeks. One-fourth of this time is given to Latin and Greek; one-fifth, to English and modern languages; one-sixth, to history and social science; one-seventh, to natural science; one-eighth to mathematics, and one-eighth to philosophy and pedagogy.
> In addition to these students in the South, Negroes have attended Northern colleges for many years. As early as 1826 one was graduated from Bowdoin College, and from that time till to-day nearly every year has seen elsewhere, other such graduates. They have, of course, met much color prejudice. Fifty years ago very few colleges would admit them at all. Even to-day no Negro has ever been admitted to Princeton, and at some other leading institutions they are rather endured than encouraged. Oberlin was the great pioneer in the work of blotting out the color line in colleges, and has more Negro graduates by far than any other Northern college.

C. They weaken his claim because numbers make his argument less specific and less testable.
D. They mainly provide background dates without showing whether education had results.

Knowledge of life and its wider meaning, has been the point of the Negro's deepest ignorance, and the sending out of teachers whose training has not been simply for bread winning, but also for human culture, has been of inestimable value in the training of these men.

FAST-style question: Which evidence most directly supports Du Bois’s claim about the function of college-trained Black leaders?

A. The figures mainly show that Du Bois has stopped discussing leadership and education.
B. The figures prove only that colleges existed, without connecting graduates to public work.
C. The figures are interesting background details, but they do not relate to the author’s claim.
**D. The figures connect college training to graduates’ work, making the claim concrete instead of abstract.**

Evidence points: These figures illustrate vividly the function of the college-bred Negro.

#### Card 20: Central idea with strong evidence
- Location: paragraphs 19-21
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> "We are to-day deliberately rearing millions of our citizens in ignorance, and at the same time limiting the rights of citizenship by educational qualifications. This is unjust. Half the black youth of the land have no opportunities open to them for learning to read, write and cipher. In the discussion as to the proper training of Negro children after they leave the public schools, we have forgotten that they are not yet decently provided with public schools.
> "Propositions are beginning to be made in the South to reduce the already meagre school facilities of Negroes. We congratulate the South on resisting, as much as it has, this pressure, and on the many millions it has spent on Negro education. But it is only fair to point out that Negro taxes and the Negroes' share of the income from indirect taxes and endowments have fully repaid this expenditure, so that the Negro public school system has not in all probability cost the white taxpayers a single cent since the war.
> The truth of this has been strikingly shown in the marked improvement of white teachers in the South. Twenty years ago the rank and file of white public school teachers were not as good as the Negro teachers. But they, by scholarships and good salaries, have been encouraged to thorough normal and collegiate preparation, while the Negro teachers have been discouraged by starvation wages and the idea that any training will do for a black teacher. If carpenters are needed it is well and good to train men as carpenters. But to train men as carpenters, and then set them to teaching is wasteful and criminal; and to train men as teachers and then refuse them living wages, unless they become carpenters, is rank nonsense.

FAST-style question: Which evidence best supports Du Bois’s point about proper training?

**A. He argues that training must fit the work, so teachers need teacher preparation rather than unrelated trade training.**
B. He mentions children generally, but that detail does not explain what kind of training teachers need.
C. He refers to schooling without showing how preparation should connect to the actual job.
D. He uses a question form, though the question alone does not prove the point about training.

Evidence points: The truth of this has been strikingly shown in the marked improvement of white teachers in the South. | proper training of Negro children after they leave the public schools, we have forgotten that they are not yet decently provided with public schools | In the discussion as to the proper training of Negro children after they leave the public schools, we have forgotten that they are not yet decently provided with...

#### Card 21: Central idea with strong evidence
- Location: paragraphs 9-11
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Was the work of these college founders successful; did it stand the test of time? Did the college graduates, with all their fine theories of life, really live? Are they useful men helping to civilize and elevate their less fortunate fellows? Let us see. Omitting all institutions which have not actually graduated students from a college course, there are to-day in the United States thirty-four institutions giving something above high school training to Negroes and designed especially for this race.
> Three of these were established in border States before the War; thirteen were planted by the Freedmen's Bureau in the years 1864-1869; nine were established between 1870 and 1880 by various church bodies; five were established after 1881 by Negro churches, and four are state institutions supported by United States' agricultural funds. In most cases the college departments are small adjuncts to high and common school work. As a matter of fact six institutions--Atlanta, Fisk, Howard, Shaw, Wilberforce and Leland, are the important Negro colleges so far as actual work and number of students are concerned. In all these institutions, seven hundred and fifty Negro college students are enrolled. In grade the best of these colleges are about a year behind the smaller New England colleges and a typical curriculum is that of Atlanta University. Here students from the grammar grades, after a three years' high school course, take a college course of 136 weeks. One-fourth of this time is given to Latin and Greek; one-fifth, to English and modern languages; one-sixth, to history and social science; one-seventh, to natural science; one-eighth to mathematics, and one-eighth to philosophy and pedagogy.
> In addition to these students in the South, Negroes have attended Northern colleges for many years. As early as 1826 one was graduated from Bowdoin College, and from that time till to-day nearly every year has seen elsewhere, other such graduates. They have, of course, met much color prejudice. Fifty years ago very few colleges would admit them at all. Even to-day no Negro has ever been admitted to Princeton, and at some other leading institutions they are rather endured than encouraged. Oberlin was the great pioneer in the work of blotting out the color line in colleges, and has more Negro graduates by far than any other Northern college.

FAST-style question: Which evidence best supports Du Bois’s claim that college education produced practical results?

**A. The passage gives figures about institutions, graduates, and occupations to show what college-trained students did.**
B. The passage mentions that some people questioned education without showing what graduates accomplished.
C. The passage names the United States, but that detail alone does not prove the effect of college education.
D. The passage includes older dates, though the dates by themselves do not show practical outcomes.

Evidence points: and four are state institutions supported by United States' agricultural funds. | there are to-day in the United States thirty-four institutions giving something above high school training to Negroes and designed especially for this race | Three of these were established in border States before the War; thirteen were planted by the Freedmen's Bureau in the years 1864-1869; nine

### ELA.9.R.2.4 — Opposing arguments, claims, evidence, and validity

#### Card 1: Compare evidence development
- Location: paired excerpts: city migration / higher education data
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington argues that schooling often teaches students about cities but “almost nothing about the country,” making it natural for students to leave rural districts.
> Passage B: Du Bois argues that Black enrollment in secondary and higher education must increase greatly to match the national average.

FAST-style question: How is the evidence in the two passages different?

A. Washington uses national enrollment data, while Du Bois discusses only rural migration.
B. Both writers use the same statistic to prove the same solution.
C. Neither writer gives support that connects to education.
**D. Washington uses a cause-and-effect example, while Du Bois uses comparison data to show a need.**

Evidence points: Washington uses cause and effect about schooling and migration. | Du Bois uses comparison data about educational access.

#### Card 2: Compare evidence development
- Location: paired excerpts: trade list / college-trained staff list
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington supports industrial education by listing practical trades: carpenters, blacksmiths, wheelwrights, brick masons, engineers, cooks, laundresses, sewing women, and housekeepers.
> Passage B: Du Bois supports higher education by listing college-trained people helping at Tuskegee, including graduates of Harvard, Oberlin, Atlanta University, Fisk, and Smith.

FAST-style question: Which comparison best explains how both writers develop their claims?

A. Both rely only on personal feelings and avoid giving examples. The passage points to a different effect.
B. Washington lists colleges, while Du Bois lists plantation trades. This does not explain the author’s move.
**C. Both use lists of examples, but Washington lists trades while Du Bois lists educated leaders and teachers.**
D. Both argue that no form of education needs trained teachers. The development of the passage points elsewhere.

Evidence points: Washington lists practical trades. | Du Bois lists college-trained professionals.

#### Card 3: Evaluate argument effectiveness
- Location: paired excerpts: economic foundation / leadership claim
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington argues that a race rises by building an economic foundation through honorable labor, saving, and ownership.
> Passage B: Du Bois argues that the “Talented Tenth” must become leaders of thought and culture because “work alone will not do it.”

FAST-style question: Which statement best evaluates the effectiveness of the two arguments?

**A. Washington is effective for practical self-sufficiency, while Du Bois is effective for leadership and long-term advancement.**
B. Washington is effective only because he rejects work, while Du Bois rejects education.
C. Both arguments are ineffective because neither gives a clear goal.
D. Du Bois is effective for farming advice, while Washington focuses only on colleges.

Evidence points: Washington appeals to practical stability. | Du Bois appeals to leadership and long-term development.

#### Card 4: Evaluate argument effectiveness
- Location: paired excerpts: trade examples / Tuskegee staff examples
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington lists practical trades learned through training.
> Passage B: Du Bois lists college-trained people working at Tuskegee and argues that Washington’s own school depends on educated teachers, scientists, historians, and leaders.

FAST-style question: Which argument is more effective against the idea that higher education is unnecessary?

A. Washington’s argument, because his list proves colleges do not need teachers. The passage points to a different effect.
**B. Du Bois’s argument, because he shows that even industrial schools depend on highly educated leaders and teachers.**
C. Du Bois’s argument, because he ignores Washington’s school completely. This does not explain the author’s move.
D. Washington’s argument, because naming trades proves higher education can never help anyone. The development of the passage points elsewhere.

Evidence points: Washington makes practical training concrete. | Du Bois uses Washington’s own institution as evidence for higher education.

#### Card 5: Evaluate claim validity
- Location: paired excerpts: economic foundation / work plus intelligence
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington argues that races rise by building an economic foundation through work, saving, and ownership.
> Passage B: Du Bois responds that “work alone will not do it” unless guided by intelligence and ideals.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois’s claim affect the validity of Washington’s claim?

A. It proves work has no value because Du Bois rejects every kind of labor.
B. It strengthens Washington’s claim by saying education should teach only manual tasks.
C. It changes the topic to farming without addressing work or education.
**D. It does not reject work, but it shows work is incomplete without intelligence and ideals.**

Evidence points: Washington supports work as a foundation. | Du Bois qualifies work by adding intelligence and ideals.

#### Card 6: Evaluate claim validity
- Location: paired excerpts: practical need / teacher need
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington argues that practical education matters because many students were trained “in everything but farming,” even though most Black people in Southern states lived in country districts.
> Passage B: Du Bois argues that the race needs teachers and colleges because first-class schools must train teachers and leaders.

FAST-style question: Which evaluation of the two claims is best supported?

A. Neither claim is valid because neither writer gives a reason for education.
**B. Both claims are valid in context because each connects its solution to a specific community need.**
C. Only Washington’s claim is valid because Du Bois never explains why schools need teachers.
D. Only Du Bois’s claim is valid because Washington never connects education to student lives.

Evidence points: Washington connects education to likely work and location. | Du Bois connects education to teacher preparation and leadership.

#### Card 7: Identify opposing claims
- Location: paired excerpts: industrial foundation / work plus ideals
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington argues that Black Americans needed to learn “the difference between being worked and working.” He says “working means civilization” and that races rise by “laying an economic foundation.”
> Passage B: Du Bois argues that “work alone will not do it unless inspired by the right ideals and guided by intelligence.” He says education “must not simply teach work—it must teach Life.”

FAST-style question: Which statement best identifies the difference between Washington’s and Du Bois’s claims?

A. Washington rejects work entirely, while Du Bois says education should teach only manual labor. The passage points to a different effect.
B. Both writers claim that higher education has no role in Black advancement. This does not explain the author’s move.
C. Both writers argue that economic development is impossible after slavery. The development of the passage points elsewhere.
**D. Washington emphasizes industrial work as a foundation, while Du Bois argues work must be guided by broader education.**

Evidence points: Washington centers work and economic foundation. | Du Bois argues work needs ideals, intelligence, and broader education.

#### Card 8: Identify opposing claims
- Location: paired excerpts: practical schooling / higher education
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington says “mere book education” can leave young people without practical preparation. He argues that students need academic education combined with useful training.
> Passage B: Du Bois says the charge of too much higher education is “baseless.” He argues that Black communities need teachers, normal schools, and colleges.

FAST-style question: What is the central disagreement between these two excerpts?

**A. Washington warns against impractical schooling, while Du Bois defends higher education as necessary for leadership and teaching.**
B. Washington says students should avoid all training, while Du Bois says schools should teach no academic subjects.
C. Both writers argue that schools should stop preparing teachers.
D. Both writers claim practical training and higher education are exactly the same.

Evidence points: Washington warns against schooling without practical preparation. | Du Bois defends higher education as necessary for teachers and leaders.

#### Card 9: Response to counterclaim
- Location: paired excerpts: practical education claim / higher education qualification
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington argues that practical education helps students prepare for useful work and economic independence.
> Passage B: Du Bois answers that practical work needs leaders, teachers, intelligence, and ideals; education must teach more than work alone.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois qualify Washington’s argument for practical education?

**A. He accepts that work matters but argues that work must be guided by intelligence, ideals, and trained leaders.**
B. He treats work as useful only when it is separated from leadership and intellectual training.
C. He agrees with Washington by making practical skill the complete goal of education.
D. He shifts the focus from leadership to economic independence without qualifying Washington’s claim.

Evidence points: Washington makes a practical education claim. | Du Bois responds by qualifying practical work with leadership and intellectual training.

#### Card 10: Response to counterclaim
- Location: paired excerpts: practical schooling concern / Tuskegee rebuttal
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington warns that schooling can separate students from useful work they are fitted to do.
> Passage B: Du Bois answers criticism of higher training by showing that Washington’s own institution relies on college-trained teachers, scientists, historians, and leaders.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois use Tuskegee as a response to criticism of higher education?

A. He shows that practical institutions should measure success only by the number of trades offered.
B. He agrees that practical schools matter, but treats college training as separate from their success.
C. He argues that industrial education is useful only when it avoids academic leadership.
**D. He shows that higher education strengthens practical institutions by preparing their teachers and leaders.**

Evidence points: Washington raises a concern about impractical education. | Du Bois uses Tuskegee as evidence for higher training.

#### Card 11: Response to counterclaim
- Location: paired excerpts: work as foundation / work alone is incomplete
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington argues that industrial education and work create the wealth and foundation from which higher education and opportunity can come.
> Passage B: Du Bois responds that work alone will not uplift a people unless it is guided by intelligence, ideals, and trained leaders.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois respond to the claim that work is the main path upward?

A. He agrees completely and says education should teach only work.
B. He rejects work by saying labor has no value for any community.
**C. He qualifies it by arguing that work needs intelligence, ideals, and leadership to become uplift.**
D. He changes the issue from education to a story with no argument.

Evidence points: Washington gives work a foundational role. | Du Bois qualifies work rather than dismissing it.

### ELA.9.R.3.2 — Paraphrase grade-level text

#### Card 1: Chunk complex syntax
- Location: opening claim excerpt
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> The Negro race, like all races, is going to be saved by its exceptional men. The problem of education, then, among Negroes must first of all deal with the Talented Tenth.

FAST-style question: Which option best paraphrases Du Bois’s claim in this excerpt?

A. Du Bois argues that only a small group deserves education and everyone else should be ignored.
B. Du Bois argues that all social problems can be solved without education.
**C. Du Bois argues that education should first develop highly capable leaders who can help lift the race.**
D. Du Bois argues that leadership matters less than job training for every student.

Evidence points: like all races | saved by its exceptional men | education... must first of all deal with the Talented Tenth

#### Card 2: Paraphrase claim or theme
- Location: central claim excerpt
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> The Negro race, like all races, is going to be saved by its exceptional men. The problem of education, then, among Negroes must first of all deal with the Talented Tenth; it is the problem of developing the best of this race that they may guide the mass away from the contamination and death of the worst.

FAST-style question: Which statement best paraphrases Du Bois’s claim?

A. Du Bois claims that no leader can affect the progress of a race.
**B. Du Bois claims that the progress of the race depends on developing exceptional leaders.**
C. Du Bois claims that only physical labor can improve society.
D. Du Bois claims that education should avoid leadership and focus only on obedience.

Evidence points: like all races | saved by its exceptional men

### ELA.9.V.1.1 — Academic vocabulary

#### Card 1: Academic tone and precision
- Location: education claim excerpt
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> The problem of education, then, among Negroes must first of all deal with the Talented Tenth; it is the problem of developing the best of this race that they may guide the mass away from the contamination and death of the worst.

**A. Lead, because Du Bois describes educated people helping direct the progress of others.**
B. Hide, because Du Bois describes keeping educated people away from others.

> The Negro race, like all races, is going to be saved by its exceptional men. The problem of education, then, among Negroes must first of all deal with the Talented Tenth; it is the problem of developing the best of this race that they may guide the mass away from the contamination and death of the worst.

FAST-style question: What does “exceptional” mean as it is used in this claim?

**A. Unusually capable, because Du Bois says these people can be developed to guide others.**
B. Completely ordinary, because he says they are the same as everyone else.
C. Physically separated, because he says they should move away from society.

#### Card 3: Word family relationship
- Location: education claim excerpt

> The problem of education, then, among Negroes must first of all deal with the Talented Tenth; it is the problem of developing the best of this race that they may guide the mass away from the contamination and death of the worst.

FAST-style question: Which related verb best fits the meaning of “developing” in this excerpt?

**A. Develop, because Du Bois is describing how education can build people’s ability to lead.**
B. Delay, because Du Bois is arguing that education should be postponed.
C. Deliver, because Du Bois is focusing on physically moving people somewhere.
D. Divide, because Du Bois is explaining how to separate schools from leaders.

### ELA.9.R.1.3 — Narrator perspective, irony, and satire

#### Card 1: Irony through contrast or reversal
- Location: paragraph 20
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> I profess in the sincerity of my heart, that I have not the least personal interest in endeavouring to promote this necessary work, having no other motive than the publick good of my country, by advancing our trade, providing for infants, relieving the poor, and giving some pleasure to the rich. I have no children, by which I can propose to get a single penny; the youngest being nine years old, and my wife past child-bearing.

FAST-style question: How does the speaker’s claim of public concern create irony?

A. The claim proves that the speaker has offered a compassionate plan for the poor.
B. The claim removes irony because the speaker clearly explains his personal profit.
C. The claim shifts the passage away from criticism and toward personal confession only.
**D. The claim sounds noble, but the reader understands that the proposal has treated people as commodities.**

Evidence points: and giving some pleasure to the rich. | publick good of my country

#### Card 2: Irony through contrast or reversal
- Location: paragraph 7
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> I have been assured by a very knowing American of my acquaintance in London, that a young healthy child well nursed, is, at a year old, a most delicious nourishing and wholesome food, whether stewed, roasted, baked, or boiled; and I make no doubt that it will equally serve in a fricasee, or a ragoust.

FAST-style question: How does the speaker’s calm wording create irony in the excerpt?

A. The speaker uses emotional language to show that he is horrified by his own idea.
**B. The speaker presents a horrifying idea as reasonable, so the reader recognizes the gap between tone and meaning.**
C. The speaker admits that his proposal is weak and expects readers to reject it.
D. The speaker changes the topic so the reader no longer thinks about poverty.

Evidence points: young healthy child well nursed | delicious nourishing and wholesome food | stewed, roasted, baked, or boiled

#### Card 3: Irony through contrast or reversal
- Location: paragraph 9
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Infant’s flesh will be in season throughout the year, but more plentiful in March, and a little before and after; for we are told by a grave author, an eminent French physician, that fish being a prolifick dyet, there are more children born in Roman Catholick countries about nine months after Lent, than at any other season; therefore, reckoning a year after Lent, the markets will be more glutted than usual, because the number of Popish infants, is at least three to one in this kingdom, and therefore it will have one other collateral advantage, by lessening the number of Papists among us.

FAST-style question: How does the speaker’s phrase “collateral advantage” create irony?

A. It shows that the speaker has stopped using economic logic and now speaks with compassion. The passage points to a different effect.
B. It proves that the speaker is mainly concerned with the health of infants. This does not explain the author’s move.
**C. It treats human suffering as a side benefit, exposing the gap between the speaker’s calm logic and the cruelty of the idea.**
D. It makes the passage less critical because it avoids mentioning any social problem. The development of the passage points elsewhere.

Evidence points: and therefore it will have one other collateral advantage, by lessening the number of Papists among us. | Infant’s flesh will be in season throughout the year | but more plentiful in March

#### Card 4: Satire through exaggeration or false logic
- Location: paragraph 16
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Sixthly, This would be a great inducement to marriage, which all wise nations have either encouraged by rewards, or enforced by laws and penalties. It would encrease the care and tenderness of mothers towards their children, when they were sure of a settlement for life to the poor babes, provided in some sort by the publick, to their annual profit instead of expence. We should soon see an honest emulation among the married women, which of them could bring the fattest child to the market. Men would become as fond of their wives, during the time of their pregnancy, as they are now of their mares in foal, their cows in calf, or sows when they are ready to farrow; nor offer to beat or kick them (as is too frequent a practice) for fear of a miscarriage.

FAST-style question: How does the speaker’s description of families and profit create satire?

A. It praises parents for becoming more loving without any economic pressure.
B. It argues that poverty is solved when families spend less time together.
C. It changes the focus from poverty to farming techniques only.
**D. It mocks the idea that family suffering can be solved by turning children into market goods.**

Evidence points: We should soon see an honest emulation among the married women, which of them could bring the fattest child to the market. | It would encrease the care and tenderness of mothers towards their children | when they were sure of a settlement for life to the poor babes, provided in some sort by the publick, to their annual profit instead of expence.

#### Card 5: Satire through exaggeration or false logic
- Location: paragraph 20
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> I profess in the sincerity of my heart, that I have not the least personal interest in endeavouring to promote this necessary work, having no other motive than the publick good of my country, by advancing our trade, providing for infants, relieving the poor, and giving some pleasure to the rich. I have no children, by which I can propose to get a single penny; the youngest being nine years old, and my wife past child-bearing.

> I do therefore humbly offer it to publick consideration, that of the hundred and twenty thousand children, already computed, twenty thousand may be reserved for breed, whereof only one fourth part to be males; which is more than we allow to sheep, black cattle, or swine, and my reason is, that these children are seldom the fruits of marriage, a circumstance not much regarded by our savages, therefore, one male will be sufficient to serve four females. That the remaining hundred thousand may, at a year old, be offered in sale to the persons of quality and fortune, through the kingdom, always advising the mother to let them suck plentifully in the last month, so as to render them plump, and fat for a good table. A child will make two dishes at an entertainment for friends, and when the family dines alone, the fore or hind quarter will make a reasonable dish, and seasoned with a little pepper or salt, will be very good boiled on the fourth day, especially in winter.

FAST-style question: How does comparing children to livestock create satire?

A. It shows that the speaker values children because he compares them to useful animals.
**B. It exaggerates economic language to expose the cruelty of treating human beings like property.**
C. It proves that livestock are the real subject of the passage.
D. It softens the proposal by making it sound natural and harmless.

Evidence points: A child will make two dishes at an entertainment for friends | I do therefore humbly offer it to publick consideration, that of the hundred and twenty thousand children, already computed, twenty thousand | and my reason is, that these children are seldom the fruits of marriage, a circumstance not much regarded by our savages, therefore, one male will be su...

#### Card 8: Satire through exaggeration or false logic
- Location: paragraph 9

Excerpt:

> Infant’s flesh will be in season throughout the year, but more plentiful in March, and a little before and after; for we are told by a grave author, an eminent French physician, that fish being a prolifick dyet, there are more children born in Roman Catholick countries about nine months after Lent, than at any other season; therefore, reckoning a year after Lent, the markets will be more glutted than usual, because the number of Popish infants, is at least three to one in this kingdom, and therefore it will have one other collateral advantage, by lessening the number of Papists among us.

FAST-style question: How does the phrase “collateral advantage” help create satire?

A. It shows that the speaker is offering a gentle compromise for poor families.

#### Card 1: Cause and effect structure

Excerpt:

> Thirdly, Whereas the maintainance of a hundred thousand children, from two years old, and upwards, cannot be computed at less than ten shillings a piece per annum, the nation’s stock will be thereby encreased fifty thousand pounds per annum, besides the profit of a new dish, introduced to the tables of all gentlemen of fortune in the kingdom, who have any refinement in taste. And the money will circulate among our selves, the goods being entirely of our own growth and manufacture.
> Fifthly, This food would likewise bring great custom to taverns, where the vintners will certainly be so prudent as to procure the best receipts for dressing it to perfection; and consequently have their houses frequented by all the fine gentlemen, who justly value themselves upon their knowledge in good eating; and a skilful cook, who understands how to oblige his guests, will contrive to make it as expensive as they please.
> Sixthly, This would be a great inducement to marriage, which all wise nations have either encouraged by rewards, or enforced by laws and penalties. It would encrease the care and tenderness of mothers towards their children, when they were sure of a settlement for life to the poor babes, provided in some sort by the publick, to their annual profit instead of expence. We should soon see an honest emulation among the married women, which of them could bring the fattest child to the market. Men would become as fond of their wives, during the time of their pregnancy, as they are now of their mares in foal, their cows in calf, or sows when they are ready to farrow; nor offer to beat or kick them (as is too frequent a practice) for fear of a miscarriage.

Thus the squire will learn to be a good landlord, and grow popular among his tenants, the mother will have eight shillings neat profit, and be fit for work till she produces another child.
