#### Card 7: Compare and contrast structure
- Location: paragraphs 1-3
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> It is a melancholy object to those, who walk through this great town, or travel in the country, when they see the streets, the roads, and cabbin-doors crowded with beggars of the female sex, followed by three, four, or six children, all in rags, and importuning every passenger for an alms. These mothers, instead of being able to work for their honest livelihood, are forced to employ all their time in stroling to beg sustenance for their helpless infants who, as they grow up, either turn thieves for want of work, or leave their dear native country, to fight for the Pretender in Spain, or sell themselves to the Barbadoes.
> I think it is agreed by all parties, that this prodigious number of children in the arms, or on the backs, or at the heels of their mothers, and frequently of their fathers, is in the present deplorable state of the kingdom, a very great additional grievance; and therefore whoever could find out a fair, cheap and easy method of making these children sound and useful members of the commonwealth, would deserve so well of the publick, as to have his statue set up for a preserver of the nation.
> But my intention is very far from being confined to provide only for the children of professed beggars: it is of a much greater extent, and shall take in the whole number of infants at a certain age, who are born of parents in effect as little able to support them, as those who demand our charity in the streets.

FAST-style question: How does Swift use contrast to frame the social problem at the beginning of the proposal?

**A. He contrasts what poor mothers should do with what poverty forces them to do, making the problem urgent.**
B. He contrasts two wealthy groups to show that poverty is not part of the issue at all.
C. He shows that mothers and children have identical choices and equal opportunities in society.
D. He shifts away from the problem by describing a peaceful family scene without public concern.

Evidence points: But my intention is very far from being confined to provide only for the children of professed beggars: it is of a much greater extent

#### Card 8: Compare and contrast structure
- Location: paragraphs 3-5
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> But my intention is very far from being confined to provide only for the children of professed beggars: it is of a much greater extent, and shall take in the whole number of infants at a certain age, who are born of parents in effect as little able to support them, as those who demand our charity in the streets.
> As to my own part, having turned my thoughts for many years upon this important subject, and maturely weighed the several schemes of our projectors, I have always found them grossly mistaken in their computation. It is true, a child just dropt from its dam, may be supported by her milk, for a solar year, with little other nourishment: at most not above the value of two shillings, which the mother may certainly get, or the value in scraps, by her lawful occupation of begging; and it is exactly at one year old that I propose to provide for them in such a manner, as, instead of being a charge upon their parents, or the parish, or wanting food and raiment for the rest of their lives, they shall, on the contrary, contribute to the feeding, and partly to the clothing of many thousands.
> There is likewise another great advantage in my scheme, that it will prevent those voluntary abortions, and that horrid practice of women murdering their bastard children, alas! too frequent among us, sacrificing the poor innocent babes, I doubt, more to avoid the expence than the shame, which would move tears and pity in the most savage and inhuman breast.

FAST-style question: How does the contrast between a limited plan and a wider plan develop Swift’s argument?

A. It narrows the problem so only a few visible families are included in the proposal.
**B. It expands the problem from visible beggars to many poor families, preparing readers for a broader proposal.**
C. It proves that the speaker has decided not to offer a proposal or discuss poverty.
D. It changes the topic from poverty to college education without connecting the ideas.

Evidence points: But my intention is very far from being confined to provide only for the children of professed beggars: it is of a much greater extent

Excerpt:

> I do therefore humbly offer it to publick consideration, that of the hundred and twenty thousand children, already computed, twenty thousand may be reserved for breed, whereof only one fourth part to be males; which is more than we allow to sheep, black cattle, or swine, and my reason is, that these children are seldom the fruits of marriage, a circumstance not much regarded by our savages, therefore, one male will be sufficient to serve four females. That the remaining hundred thousand may, at a year old, be offered in sale to the persons of quality and fortune, through the kingdom, always advising the mother to let them suck plentifully in the last month, so as to render them plump, and fat for a good table. A child will make two dishes at an entertainment for friends, and when the family dines alone, the fore or hind quarter will make a reasonable dish, and seasoned with a little pepper or salt, will be very good boiled on the fourth day, especially in winter.
> Infant’s flesh will be in season throughout the year, but more plentiful in March, and a little before and after; for we are told by a grave author, an eminent French physician, that fish being a prolifick dyet, there are more children born in Roman Catholick countries about nine months after Lent, than at any other season; therefore, reckoning a year after Lent, the markets will be more glutted than usual, because the number of Popish infants, is at least three to one in this kingdom, and therefore it will have one other collateral advantage, by lessening the number of Papists among us.

> Many other advantages might be enumerated. But this, and many others, I omit, being studious of brevity.
> Supposing that one thousand families in this city, would be constant customers for infants flesh, besides others who might have it at merry meetings, particularly at weddings and christenings, I compute that Dublin would take off annually about twenty thousand carcasses; and the rest of the kingdom (where probably they will be sold somewhat cheaper) the remaining eighty thousand.
> But, as to myself, having been wearied out for many years with offering vain, idle, visionary thoughts, and at length utterly despairing of success, I fortunately fell upon this proposal, which, as it is wholly new, so it hath something solid and real, of no expence and little trouble, full in our own power, and whereby we can incur no danger in disobliging England. For this kind of commodity will not bear exportation, and flesh being of too tender a consistence, to admit a long continuance in salt, although perhaps I could name a country, which would be glad to eat up our whole nation without it.

I have no children, by which I can propose to get a single penny; the youngest being nine years old, and my wife past child-bearing.

> Some persons of a desponding spirit are in great concern about that vast number of poor people, who are aged, diseased, or maimed; and I have been desired to employ my thoughts what course may be taken, to ease the nation of so grievous an incumbrance. And as to the young labourers, they are now in almost as hopeful a condition. Excerpt:

> Thirdly, Whereas the maintainance of a hundred thousand children, from two years old, and upwards, cannot be computed at less than ten shillings a piece per annum, the nation’s stock will be thereby encreased fifty thousand pounds per annum, besides the profit of a new dish, introduced to the tables of all gentlemen of fortune in the kingdom, who have any refinement in taste. And the money will circulate among our selves, the goods being entirely of our own growth and manufacture.
> Fifthly, This food would likewise bring great custom to taverns, where the vintners will certainly be so prudent as to procure the best receipts for dressing it to perfection; and consequently have their houses frequented by all the fine gentlemen, who justly value themselves upon their knowledge in good eating; and a skilful cook, who understands how to oblige his guests, will contrive to make it as expensive as they please.
> Sixthly, This would be a great inducement to marriage, which all wise nations have either encouraged by rewards, or enforced by laws and penalties. It would encrease the care and tenderness of mothers towards their children, when they were sure of a settlement for life to the poor babes, provided in some sort by the publick, to their annual profit instead of expence. We should soon see an honest emulation among the married women, which of them could bring the fattest child to the market. Men would become as fond of their wives, during the time of their pregnancy, as they are now of their mares in foal, their cows in calf, or sows when they are ready to farrow; nor offer to beat or kick them (as is too frequent a practice) for fear of a miscarriage.

Evidence points: will be thereby encreased fifty thousand pounds per annum, besides the profit of a new dish, introduced to the tables of all gentlemen of fortune in the kingdom, who have any refinement in taste

> It is a melancholy object to those, who walk through this great town, or travel in the country, when they see the streets, the roads, and cabbin-doors crowded with beggars of the female sex, followed by three, four, or six children, all in rags, and importuning every passenger for an alms. These mothers, instead of being able to work for their honest livelihood, are forced to employ all their time in stroling to beg sustenance for their helpless infants who, as they grow up, either turn thieves for want of work, or leave their dear native country, to fight for the Pretender in Spain, or sell themselves to the Barbadoes.
> I think it is agreed by all parties, that this prodigious number of children in the arms, or on the backs, or at the heels of their mothers, and frequently of their fathers, is in the present deplorable state of the kingdom, a very great additional grievance; and therefore whoever could find out a fair, cheap and easy method of making these children sound and useful members of the commonwealth, would deserve so well of the publick, as to have his statue set up for a preserver of the nation.
> But my intention is very far from being confined to provide only for the children of professed beggars: it is of a much greater extent, and shall take in the whole number of infants at a certain age, who are born of parents in effect as little able to support them, as those who demand our charity in the streets.

> I have already computed the charge of nursing a beggar’s child (in which list I reckon all cottagers, labourers, and four-fifths of the farmers) to be about two shillings per annum, rags included; and I believe no gentleman would repine to give ten shillings for the carcass of a good fat child, which, as I have said, will make four dishes of excellent nutritive meat, when he hath only some particular friend, or his own family to dine with him. Thus the squire will learn to be a good landlord, and grow popular among his tenants, the mother will have eight shillings neat profit, and be fit for work till she produces another child.
> But in order to justify my friend, he confessed, that this expedient was put into his head by the famous Psalmanaazor, a native of the island Formosa, who came from thence to London, above twenty years ago, and in conversation told my friend, that in his country, when any young person happened to be put to death, the executioner sold the carcass to persons of quality, as a prime dainty; and that, in his time, the body of a plump girl of fifteen, who was crucified for an attempt to poison the Emperor, was sold to his imperial majesty’s prime minister of state, and other great mandarins of the court in joints from the gibbet, at four hundred crowns. Neither indeed can I deny, that if the same use were made of several plump young girls in this town, who without one single groat to their fortunes, cannot stir abroad without a chair, and appear at a playhouse and assemblies in foreign fineries which they never will pay for, the kingdom would not be the worse.

#### Card 1: Ethos: credibility and authority
- Location: paragraphs 12-13
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> As to our City of Dublin, shambles may be appointed for this purpose, in the most convenient parts of it, and butchers we may be assured will not be wanting; although I rather recommend buying the children alive, and dressing them hot from the knife, as we do roasting pigs.
> A very worthy person, a true lover of his country, and whose virtues I highly esteem, was lately pleased in discoursing on this matter, to offer a refinement upon my scheme. He said, that many gentlemen of this kingdom, having of late destroyed their deer, he conceived that the want of venison might be well supplied by the bodies of young lads and maidens, not exceeding fourteen years of age, nor under twelve; so great a number of both sexes in every county being now ready to starve for want of work and service: and these to be disposed of by their parents if alive, or otherwise by their nearest relations. But with due deference to so excellent a friend, and so deserving a patriot, I cannot be altogether in his sentiments; for as to the males, my American acquaintance assured me from frequent experience, that their flesh was generally tough and lean, like that of our schoolboys, by continual exercise, and their taste disagreeable, and to fatten them would not answer the charge. Then as to the females, it would, I think, with humble submission, be a loss to the publick, because they soon would become breeders themselves: and besides, it is not improbable that some scrupulous people might be apt to censure such a practice, (although indeed very unjustly) as a little bordering upon cruelty, which, I confess, hath always been with me the strongest objection against any project, how well soever intended.

FAST-style question: How does the description of the “very worthy person” affect the speaker’s credibility?

A. It presents the source as foolish and unconnected to the proposal.
**B. It presents the source as patriotic and respectable, making the refinement seem more trustworthy.**
C. It shows the speaker refuses to consider ideas from anyone else.
D. It makes the passage rely only on setting rather than credibility.

Evidence points: A very worthy person, a true lover of his country

#### Card 2: Ethos: credibility and authority
- Location: paragraphs 24-25
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> After all, I am not so violently bent upon my own opinion, as to reject any offer, proposed by wise men, which shall be found equally innocent, cheap, easy, and effectual. But before something of that kind shall be advanced in contradiction to my scheme, and offering a better, I desire the author or authors will be pleased maturely to consider two points. First, As things now stand, how they will be able to find food and raiment for a hundred thousand useless mouths and backs. And secondly, There being a round million of creatures in humane figure throughout this kingdom, whose whole subsistence put into a common stock, would leave them in debt two million of pounds sterling, adding those who are beggars by profession, to the bulk of farmers, cottagers and labourers, with their wives and children, who are beggars in effect; I desire those politicians who dislike my overture, and may perhaps be so bold to attempt an answer, that they will first ask the parents of these mortals, whether they would not at this day think it a great happiness to have been sold for food at a year old, in the manner I prescribe, and thereby have avoided such a perpetual scene of misfortunes, as they have since gone through, by the oppression of landlords, the impossibility of paying rent without money or trade, the want of common sustenance, with neither house nor clothes to cover them from the inclemencies of the weather, and the most inevitable prospect of intailing the like, or greater miseries, upon their breed for ever.
> I profess in the sincerity of my heart, that I have not the least personal interest in endeavouring to promote this necessary work, having no other motive than the publick good of my country, by advancing our trade, providing for infants, relieving the poor, and giving some pleasure to the rich. I have no children, by which I can propose to get a single penny; the youngest being nine years old, and my wife past child-bearing.

FAST-style question: How does the speaker’s claim of public concern affect the satire?

A. It proves the proposal is gentle because the speaker openly admits personal profit.
B. It removes the satirical effect by making the speaker reject his own plan.
**C. It makes him sound trustworthy while exposing how cruel his supposedly selfless logic has been.**
D. It shifts the argument away from public concern and into unrelated biography.

Evidence points: no other motive than the publick good of my country, by advancing our trade, providing for infants, relieving the poor, and giving some pleasure to the rich

#### Card 3: Ethos: credibility and authority
- Location: paragraphs 7-9
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> I am assured by our merchants, that a boy or a girl, before twelve years old, is no saleable commodity, and even when they come to this age, they will not yield above three pounds, or three pounds and half a crown at most, on the exchange; which cannot turn to account either to the parents or kingdom, the charge of nutriments and rags having been at least four times that value.
> I have been assured by a very knowing American of my acquaintance in London, that a young healthy child well nursed, is, at a year old, a most delicious nourishing and wholesome food, whether stewed, roasted, baked, or boiled; and I make no doubt that it will equally serve in a fricasee, or a ragoust.
> I do therefore humbly offer it to publick consideration, that of the hundred and twenty thousand children, already computed, twenty thousand may be reserved for breed, whereof only one fourth part to be males; which is more than we allow to sheep, black cattle, or swine, and my reason is, that these children are seldom the fruits of marriage, a circumstance not much regarded by our savages, therefore, one male will be sufficient to serve four females. That the remaining hundred thousand may, at a year old, be offered in sale to the persons of quality and fortune, through the kingdom, always advising the mother to let them suck plentifully in the last month, so as to render them plump, and fat for a good table. A child will make two dishes at an entertainment for friends, and when the family dines alone, the fore or hind quarter will make a reasonable dish, and seasoned with a little pepper or salt, will be very good boiled on the fourth day, especially in winter.

Evidence points: I am assured by our merchants, that a boy or a girl, before twelve years old, is no saleable commodity

> It is a melancholy object to those, who walk through this great town, or travel in the country, when they see the streets, the roads, and cabbin-doors crowded with beggars of the female sex, followed by three, four, or six children, all in rags, and importuning every passenger for an alms. These mothers, instead of being able to work for their honest livelihood, are forced to employ all their time in stroling to beg sustenance for their helpless infants who, as they grow up, either turn thieves for want of work, or leave their dear native country, to fight for the Pretender in Spain, or sell themselves to the Barbadoes.
> I think it is agreed by all parties, that this prodigious number of children in the arms, or on the backs, or at the heels of their mothers, and frequently of their fathers, is in the present deplorable state of the kingdom, a very great additional grievance; and therefore whoever could find out a fair, cheap and easy method of making these children sound and useful members of the commonwealth, would deserve so well of the publick, as to have his statue set up for a preserver of the nation.
> But my intention is very far from being confined to provide only for the children of professed beggars: it is of a much greater extent, and shall take in the whole number of infants at a certain age, who are born of parents in effect as little able to support them, as those who demand our charity in the streets.

I again subtract fifty thousand, for those women who miscarry, or whose children die by accident or disease within the year. There only remain a hundred and twenty thousand children of poor parents annually born. The question therefore is, How this number shall be reared and provided for? which, as I have already said, under the present situation of affairs, is utterly impossible by all the methods hitherto proposed. For we can neither employ them in handicraft or agriculture; they neither build houses, (I mean in the country) nor cultivate land: they can very seldom pick up a livelihood by stealing till they arrive at six years old; except where they are of towardly parts, although I confess they learn the rudiments much earlier; during which time they can however be properly looked upon only as probationers; as I have been informed by a principal gentleman in the county of Cavan, who protested to me, that he never knew above one or two instances under the age of six, even in a part of the kingdom so renowned for the quickest proficiency in that art.

- Source status: stored

### ELA.9.R.2.3 — Rhetorical appeals and author purpose

#### Card 1: Ethos: credibility and authority
- Location: paragraph 6
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> I do not mean in any way to apologize for the curse of slavery, which was a curse to both races, but in what I say about industrial training in slavery I am simply stating facts. This training was crude, and was given for selfish purposes. It did not answer the highest ends, because there was an absence of mental training in connection with the training of the hand. To a large degree, though, this business contact with the Southern white man, and the industrial training on the plantations, left the Negro at the close of the war in possession of nearly all the common and skilled labor in the South. The industries that gave the South its power, prominence and wealth prior to the Civil War were mainly the raising of cotton, sugar cane, rice and tobacco. Before the way could be prepared for the proper growing and marketing of these crops forests had to be cleared, houses to be built, public roads and railroads constructed. In all these works the Negro did most of the heavy work. In the planting, cultivating and marketing of the crops not only was the Negro the chief dependence, but in the manufacture of tobacco he became a skilled and proficient workman, and in this, up to the present time, in the South, holds the lead in the large tobacco manufactories.

FAST-style question: How does Washington’s careful qualification help his argument?

**A. It shows fairness and limits possible objections, making readers more likely to trust his position.**
B. It shows that Washington has abandoned his position and no longer believes it.
C. It makes the passage depend only on emotion rather than credibility or reasoning.
D. It proves that readers should ignore the central issue of education.

Evidence points: I do not mean in any way to apologize for the curse of slavery

#### Card 2: Ethos: credibility and authority
- Location: paragraphs 11-13
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> I would set no limits to the attainments of the Negro in arts, in letters or statesmanship, but I believe the surest way to reach those ends is by laying the foundation in the little things of life that lie immediately about one's door. I plead for industrial education and development for the Negro not because I want to cramp him, but because I want to free him. I want to see him enter the all-powerful business and commercial world.
> It was such combined mental, moral and industrial education which the late General Armstrong set out to give at the Hampton Institute when he established that school thirty years ago. The Hampton Institute has continued along the lines laid down by its great founder, and now each year an increasing number of similar schools are being established in the South, for the people of both races.
> Early in the history of the Tuskegee Institute we began to combine industrial training with mental and moral culture. Our first efforts were in the direction of agriculture, and we began teaching this with no appliances except one hoe and a blind mule. From this small beginning we have grown until now the Institute owns two thousand acres of land, eight hundred of which are cultivated each year by the young men of the school. We began teaching wheelwrighting and blacksmithing in a small way to the men, and laundry work, cooking and sewing and housekeeping to the young women. The fourteen hundred and over young men and women who attended the school during the last school year received instruction--in addition to academic and religious training--in thirty-three trades and industries, including carpentry, blacksmithing, printing, wheelwrighting harnessmaking, painting, machinery, founding, shoemaking, brickmasonry and brickmaking, plastering, sawmilling, tinsmithing, tailoring, mechanical and architectural drawing, electrical and steam engineering, canning, sewing, dressmaking, millinery, cooking, laundering, housekeeping, mattress making, basketry, nursing, agriculture, dairying and stock raising, horticulture.

FAST-style question: How does Washington’s careful qualification help his argument?

**A. It shows fairness and limits possible objections, making readers more likely to trust his position.**
B. It shows that Washington has abandoned his position and no longer believes it.

D. It proves that readers should ignore the central issue of education.

Evidence points: I would set no limits to the attainments of the Negro in arts, in letters or statesmanship

> It has been necessary for the Negro to learn the difference between being worked and working--to learn that being worked meant degradation, while working means civilization; that all forms of labor are honorable, and all forms of idleness disgraceful. If, in too many cases, the Negro race began development at the wrong end, it was largely because neither white nor black properly understood the case. Nor is it any wonder that this was so, for never before in the history of the world had just such a problem been presented as that of the two races at the coming of freedom in this country.
> For two hundred and fifty years, I believe the way for the redemption of the Negro was being prepared through industrial development. Through all those years the Southern white man did business with the Negro in a way that no one else has done business with him. In most cases if a Southern white man wanted a house built he consulted a Negro mechanic about the plan and about the actual building of the structure. If he wanted a suit of clothes made he went to a Negro tailor, and for shoes he went to a shoemaker of the same race. In a certain way every slave plantation in the South was an industrial school. On these plantations young colored men and women were constantly being trained not only as farmers but as carpenters, blacksmiths, wheelwrights, brick masons, engineers, cooks, laundresses, sewing women and housekeepers.

It has been necessary for him to learn that all races that have got upon their feet have done so largely by laying an economic foundation, and, in general, by beginning in a proper cultivation and ownership of the soil.

**A. It frames work as necessary for freedom and progress, making industrial education feel urgent.**
B. It argues that work is unrelated to education or progress.
C. It treats freedom as a reason to avoid training and discipline.
D. It shifts the passage away from public purpose into private memory.

Evidence points: It has been necessary for the Negro to learn the difference between being worked and working--to learn that being worked meant degradation | It has been necessary for him to learn that all races that have got upon their feet have done so largely by laying an economic foundation

#### Card 6: Rhetorical appeal connected to purpose
- Location: paragraphs 3-5
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> It has been necessary for the Negro to learn the difference between being worked and working--to learn that being worked meant degradation, while working means civilization; that all forms of labor are honorable, and all forms of idleness disgraceful. It has been necessary for him to learn that all races that have got upon their feet have done so largely by laying an economic foundation, and, in general, by beginning in a proper cultivation and ownership of the soil.
> Forty years ago my race emerged from slavery into freedom. If, in too many cases, the Negro race began development at the wrong end, it was largely because neither white nor black properly understood the case. Nor is it any wonder that this was so, for never before in the history of the world had just such a problem been presented as that of the two races at the coming of freedom in this country.
> For two hundred and fifty years, I believe the way for the redemption of the Negro was being prepared through industrial development. Through all those years the Southern white man did business with the Negro in a way that no one else has done business with him. In most cases if a Southern white man wanted a house built he consulted a Negro mechanic about the plan and about the actual building of the structure. If he wanted a suit of clothes made he went to a Negro tailor, and for shoes he went to a shoemaker of the same race. In a certain way every slave plantation in the South was an industrial school. On these plantations young colored men and women were constantly being trained not only as farmers but as carpenters, blacksmiths, wheelwrights, brick masons, engineers, cooks, laundresses, sewing women and housekeepers.

FAST-style question: How does Washington’s appeal connect work to his larger purpose?

A. It argues that work is unrelated to education or progress.
B. It treats freedom as a reason to avoid training and discipline.
C. It shifts the passage away from public purpose into private memory.
**D. It frames work as necessary for freedom and progress, making industrial education feel urgent.**

Evidence points: It has been necessary for the Negro to learn the difference between being worked and working--to learn that being worked meant degradation | It has been necessary for him to learn that all races that have got upon their feet have done so largely by laying an economic foundation

#### Card 7: Logos: evidence and reasoning
- Location: paragraph 7
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> In most of the industries, though, what happened? For nearly twenty years after the war, except in a few instances, the value of the industrial training given by the plantations was overlooked. Negro men and women were educated in literature, in mathematics and in the sciences, with little thought of what had been taking place during the preceding two hundred and fifty years, except, perhaps, as something to be escaped, to be got as far away from as possible. As a generation began to pass, those who had been trained as mechanics in slavery began to disappear by death, and gradually it began to be realized that there were few to take their places. There were young men educated in foreign tongues, but few in carpentry or in mechanical or architectural drawing. Many were trained in Latin, but few as engineers and blacksmiths. Too many were taken from the farm and educated, but educated in everything but farming. For this reason they had no interest in farming and did not return to it. And yet eighty-five per cent. of the Negro population of the Southern states lives and for a considerable time will continue to live in the country districts. The charge is often brought against the members of my race--and too often justly, I confess--that they are found leaving the country districts and flocking into the great cities where temptations are more frequent and harder to resist, and where the Negro people too often become demoralized. Think, though, how frequently it is the case that from the first day that a pupil begins to go to school his books teach him much about the cities of the world and city life, and almost nothing about the country. How natural it is, then, that when he has the ordering of his life he wants to live it in the city.

FAST-style question: How does Washington’s factual reasoning support his purpose?

A. It shows that Washington wants readers to ignore practical work and focus only on theory.
**B. It connects industrial education to real conditions, making his argument seem practical rather than abstract.**
C. It turns the passage into a personal story with no larger claim about education.
D. It weakens his point by avoiding evidence about labor, training, or Southern life.

Evidence points: And yet eighty-five per cent.

#### Card 10: Rhetorical choice fits purpose
- Location: paragraph 20
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> I close, then, as I began, by saying that as a slave the Negro was worked, and that as a freeman he must learn to work. There is still doubt in many quarters as to the ability of the Negro unguided, unsupported, to hew his own path and put into visible, tangible, indisputable form, products and signs of civilization. This doubt cannot be much affected by abstract arguments, no matter how delicately and convincingly woven together. Patiently, quietly, doggedly, persistently, through summer and winter, sunshine and shadow, by self-sacrifice, by foresight, by honesty and industry, we must re-enforce argument with results. One farm bought, one house built, one home sweetly and intelligently kept, one man who is the largest tax payer or has the largest bank account, one school or church maintained, one factory running successfully, one truck garden profitably cultivated, one patient cured by a Negro doctor, one sermon well preached, one office well filled, one life cleanly lived--these will tell more in our favor than all the abstract eloquence that can be summoned to plead our cause. Our pathway must be up through the soil, up through swamps, up through forests, up through the streams, the rocks, up through commerce, education and religion!

FAST-style question: How does Washington’s closing claim fit his purpose?

B. It changes the passage into a private story without a public purpose.

**D. It returns to his main contrast between forced labor and purposeful work as preparation for progress.**

### ELA.9.R.2.4 — Opposing arguments, claims, evidence, and validity

#### Card 1: Compare evidence development
- Location: paired excerpts: city migration / higher education data
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington argues that schooling often teaches students about cities but “almost nothing about the country,” making it natural for students to leave rural districts.
> Passage B: Du Bois argues that Black enrollment in secondary and higher education must increase greatly to match the national average.

FAST-style question: How is the evidence in the two passages different?

A. Washington uses national enrollment data, while Du Bois discusses only rural migration.
B. Both writers use the same statistic to prove the same solution.
C. Neither writer gives support that connects to education.
**D. Washington uses a cause-and-effect example, while Du Bois uses comparison data to show a need.**

Evidence points: Washington uses cause and effect about schooling and migration. | Du Bois uses comparison data about educational access.

#### Card 2: Compare evidence development
- Location: paired excerpts: trade list / college-trained staff list
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington supports industrial education by listing practical trades: carpenters, blacksmiths, wheelwrights, brick masons, engineers, cooks, laundresses, sewing women, and housekeepers.
> Passage B: Du Bois supports higher education by listing college-trained people helping at Tuskegee, including graduates of Harvard, Oberlin, Atlanta University, Fisk, and Smith.

FAST-style question: Which comparison best explains how both writers develop their claims?

A. Both rely only on personal feelings and avoid giving examples. The passage points to a different effect.
B. Washington lists colleges, while Du Bois lists plantation trades. This does not explain the author’s move.
**C. Both use lists of examples, but Washington lists trades while Du Bois lists educated leaders and teachers.**
D. Both argue that no form of education needs trained teachers. The development of the passage points elsewhere.

Evidence points: Washington lists practical trades. | Du Bois lists college-trained professionals.

#### Card 3: Evaluate argument effectiveness
- Location: paired excerpts: economic foundation / leadership claim
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington argues that a race rises by building an economic foundation through honorable labor, saving, and ownership.
> Passage B: Du Bois argues that the “Talented Tenth” must become leaders of thought and culture because “work alone will not do it.”

FAST-style question: Which statement best evaluates the effectiveness of the two arguments?

**A. Washington is effective for practical self-sufficiency, while Du Bois is effective for leadership and long-term advancement.**
B. Washington is effective only because he rejects work, while Du Bois rejects education.
C. Both arguments are ineffective because neither gives a clear goal.
D. Du Bois is effective for farming advice, while Washington focuses only on colleges.

Evidence points: Washington appeals to practical stability. | Du Bois appeals to leadership and long-term development.

#### Card 4: Evaluate argument effectiveness
- Location: paired excerpts: trade examples / Tuskegee staff examples
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington lists practical trades learned through training.
> Passage B: Du Bois lists college-trained people working at Tuskegee and argues that Washington’s own school depends on educated teachers, scientists, historians, and leaders.

FAST-style question: Which argument is more effective against the idea that higher education is unnecessary?

A. Washington’s argument, because his list proves colleges do not need teachers. The passage points to a different effect.
**B. Du Bois’s argument, because he shows that even industrial schools depend on highly educated leaders and teachers.**
C. Du Bois’s argument, because he ignores Washington’s school completely. This does not explain the author’s move.
D. Washington’s argument, because naming trades proves higher education can never help anyone. The development of the passage points elsewhere.

Evidence points: Washington makes practical training concrete. | Du Bois uses Washington’s own institution as evidence for higher education.

#### Card 5: Evaluate claim validity
- Location: paired excerpts: economic foundation / work plus intelligence
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington argues that races rise by building an economic foundation through work, saving, and ownership.
> Passage B: Du Bois responds that “work alone will not do it” unless guided by intelligence and ideals.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois’s claim affect the validity of Washington’s claim?

A. It proves work has no value because Du Bois rejects every kind of labor.
B. It strengthens Washington’s claim by saying education should teach only manual tasks.
C. It changes the topic to farming without addressing work or education.
**D. It does not reject work, but it shows work is incomplete without intelligence and ideals.**

Evidence points: Washington supports work as a foundation. | Du Bois qualifies work by adding intelligence and ideals.

#### Card 6: Evaluate claim validity
- Location: paired excerpts: practical need / teacher need
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington argues that practical education matters because many students were trained “in everything but farming,” even though most Black people in Southern states lived in country districts.
> Passage B: Du Bois argues that the race needs teachers and colleges because first-class schools must train teachers and leaders.

FAST-style question: Which evaluation of the two claims is best supported?

A. Neither claim is valid because neither writer gives a reason for education.
**B. Both claims are valid in context because each connects its solution to a specific community need.**
C. Only Washington’s claim is valid because Du Bois never explains why schools need teachers.
D. Only Du Bois’s claim is valid because Washington never connects education to student lives.

Evidence points: Washington connects education to likely work and location. | Du Bois connects education to teacher preparation and leadership.

#### Card 7: Identify opposing claims
- Location: paired excerpts: industrial foundation / work plus ideals
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington argues that Black Americans needed to learn “the difference between being worked and working.” He says “working means civilization” and that races rise by “laying an economic foundation.”
> Passage B: Du Bois argues that “work alone will not do it unless inspired by the right ideals and guided by intelligence.” He says education “must not simply teach work—it must teach Life.”

FAST-style question: Which statement best identifies the difference between Washington’s and Du Bois’s claims?

A. Washington rejects work entirely, while Du Bois says education should teach only manual labor. The passage points to a different effect.
B. Both writers claim that higher education has no role in Black advancement. This does not explain the author’s move.
C. Both writers argue that economic development is impossible after slavery. The development of the passage points elsewhere.
**D. Washington emphasizes industrial work as a foundation, while Du Bois argues work must be guided by broader education.**

Evidence points: Washington centers work and economic foundation. | Du Bois argues work needs ideals, intelligence, and broader education.

#### Card 8: Identify opposing claims
- Location: paired excerpts: practical schooling / higher education
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington says “mere book education” can leave young people without practical preparation. He argues that students need academic education combined with useful training.
> Passage B: Du Bois says the charge of too much higher education is “baseless.” He argues that Black communities need teachers, normal schools, and colleges.

FAST-style question: What is the central disagreement between these two excerpts?

**A. Washington warns against impractical schooling, while Du Bois defends higher education as necessary for leadership and teaching.**
B. Washington says students should avoid all training, while Du Bois says schools should teach no academic subjects.
C. Both writers argue that schools should stop preparing teachers.
D. Both writers claim practical training and higher education are exactly the same.

Evidence points: Washington warns against schooling without practical preparation. | Du Bois defends higher education as necessary for teachers and leaders.

#### Card 9: Response to counterclaim
- Location: paired excerpts: practical education claim / higher education qualification
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington argues that practical education helps students prepare for useful work and economic independence.
> Passage B: Du Bois answers that practical work needs leaders, teachers, intelligence, and ideals; education must teach more than work alone.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois qualify Washington’s argument for practical education?

**A. He accepts that work matters but argues that work must be guided by intelligence, ideals, and trained leaders.**
B. He treats work as useful only when it is separated from leadership and intellectual training.
C. He agrees with Washington by making practical skill the complete goal of education.
D. He shifts the focus from leadership to economic independence without qualifying Washington’s claim.

Evidence points: Washington makes a practical education claim. | Du Bois responds by qualifying practical work with leadership and intellectual training.

#### Card 10: Response to counterclaim
- Location: paired excerpts: practical schooling concern / Tuskegee rebuttal
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington warns that schooling can separate students from useful work they are fitted to do.
> Passage B: Du Bois answers criticism of higher training by showing that Washington’s own institution relies on college-trained teachers, scientists, historians, and leaders.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois use Tuskegee as a response to criticism of higher education?

A. He shows that practical institutions should measure success only by the number of trades offered.
B. He agrees that practical schools matter, but treats college training as separate from their success.
C. He argues that industrial education is useful only when it avoids academic leadership.
**D. He shows that higher education strengthens practical institutions by preparing their teachers and leaders.**

Evidence points: Washington raises a concern about impractical education. | Du Bois uses Tuskegee as evidence for higher training.

#### Card 11: Response to counterclaim
- Location: paired excerpts: work as foundation / work alone is incomplete
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Washington argues that industrial education and work create the wealth and foundation from which higher education and opportunity can come.
> Passage B: Du Bois responds that work alone will not uplift a people unless it is guided by intelligence, ideals, and trained leaders.

FAST-style question: How does Du Bois respond to the claim that work is the main path upward?

A. He agrees completely and says education should teach only work.
B. He rejects work by saying labor has no value for any community.
**C. He qualifies it by arguing that work needs intelligence, ideals, and leadership to become uplift.**
D. He changes the issue from education to a story with no argument.

Evidence points: Washington gives work a foundational role. | Du Bois qualifies work rather than dismissing it.

### ELA.9.R.1.1 — Key literary elements add layers of meaning

#### Card 1: Characterization adds meaning
- Location: paragraph 137
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> When Perseus went away from the king's palace, he began to feel sorry that he had spoken so rashly. For how should he ever make good his promise and do the king's bidding? He did not know which way to go to find the Gorgons, and he had no weapon with which to slay the terrible Medusa. But at any rate he would never show his face to the king again, unless he could bring the head of terror with him. He went down to the shore and stood looking out over the sea towards Argos, his native land; and while he looked, the sun went down, and the moon arose, and a soft wind came blowing from the west. Then, all at once, two persons, a man and a woman, stood before him. Both were tall and noble. The man looked like a prince; and there were wings on his cap and on his feet, and he carried a winged staff, around which two golden serpents were twined.

FAST-style question: What does Perseus’s reaction to his promise reveal about him?

A. It shows that Perseus immediately gives up and decides to hide forever. The passage points to a different effect.
B. It reveals that Perseus already knows exactly how to defeat Medusa. This does not explain the author’s move.
C. It shows that Perseus made a careful plan before speaking to the king. The development of the passage points elsewhere.
**D. It shows that Perseus recognizes the danger of his boast but is still determined to keep his promise.**

Evidence points: But at any rate he would never show his face to the king again, unless he could bring the head of terror with him. | he began to feel sorry that he had spoken so rashly | He went down to the shore and stood looking out over the sea towards Argos, his native land; and while he looked, the sun went down, and the

And no sooner was he prone upon the couch than curious iron arms reached out and clasped his body in their embrace and held him down so that he could not move hand or foot. The wretched man shrieked and cried for mercy; but Theseus stood over him and looked him straight in the eye.

FAST-style question: What does Theseus’s treatment of the robber reveal about him?

**B. It shows that Theseus is forceful and willing to punish the robber with the same cruelty the robber used on others.**
C. It reveals that Theseus is unaware of the robber’s cruelty. This does not explain the author’s move. The development of the passage points elsewhere.
D. It shows that Theseus avoids conflict by leaving the robber alone. The development of the passage points elsewhere. This misses the evidence in the excerpt.

Evidence points: The wretched man shrieked and cried for mercy; but Theseus stood over him and looked him straight in the eye. | But I tell you, you must lie upon it | seized the trembling man around the waist and threw him by force upon the bed

### ELA.9.R.1.3 — Narrator perspective, irony, and satire

#### Card 1: Narrator perspective shapes understanding
- Location: paragraph 13
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> There was the huge tree asleep yet in the paling moonlight, and small and hopeful Sylvia began with utmost bravery to mount to the top of it, with tingling, eager blood coursing the channels of her whole frame, with her bare feet and fingers, that pinched and held like bird’s claws to the monstrous ladder reaching up, up, almost to the sky itself. First she must mount the white oak tree that grew alongside, where she was almost lost among the dark branches and the green leaves heavy and wet with dew; a bird fluttered off its nest, and a red squirrel ran to and fro and scolded pettishly at the harmless housebreaker. Sylvia felt her way easily. She had often climbed there, and knew that higher still one of the oak’s upper branches chafed against the pine trunk, just where its lower boughs were set close together. There, when she made the dangerous pass from one tree to the other, the great enterprise would really begin.

FAST-style question: How does the narrator’s focus on Sylvia’s climb shape the reader’s understanding?

A. It suggests that Sylvia is careless because she has never climbed near the tree before. The passage points to a different effect.
B. It shifts the passage away from Sylvia by making the tree the only important subject. This does not explain the author’s move.
**C. It makes the reader see the climb through Sylvia’s effort and knowledge, so the search feels personal rather than accidental.**
D. It proves that the stranger has already taught Sylvia exactly where to climb. The development of the passage points elsewhere.

Evidence points: small and hopeful Sylvia | First she must mount the white oak tree that grew alongside | knew that higher still one of the oak’s upper branches chafed against the pine trunk, just where its lower boughs were set close together

#### Card 2: Narrator perspective shapes understanding
- Location: paragraph 19
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

> The guest waked from a dream, and remembering his day’s pleasure hurried to dress himself that it might sooner begin. He was sure from the way the shy little girl looked once or twice yesterday that she had at least seen the white heron, and now she must really be persuaded to tell. Here she comes now, paler than ever, and her worn old frock is torn and tattered, and smeared with pine pitch. The grandmother and the sportsman stand in the door together and question her, and the splendid moment has come to speak of the dead hemlock-tree by the green marsh.

FAST-style question: How does the narrator’s shift to the guest’s expectation shape the reader’s understanding?

## Do People Need Small Talk to Be Happy? — Stephanie Hayes
- Source status: manual_upload
- Local source available: yes
- Word count: 702
- Official mapped standards: ELA.9.R.2.1, ELA.9.R.2.2, ELA.9.R.2.3, ELA.9.R.2.4, ELA.9.V.1.2, ELA.9.V.1.3
- Standards with gold cards: ELA.9.V.1.1, ELA.9.V.1.2, ELA.9.V.1.3
- Standards still needing cards: ELA.9.R.2.1, ELA.9.R.2.2, ELA.9.R.2.3, ELA.9.R.2.4

### ELA.9.V.1.1 — Academic vocabulary

#### Card 1: Academic tone and precision
- Location: lines 20-32
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Researchers eavesdropped on undergraduates for four days, then cataloged each overheard conversation as either “small talk” or “substantive.” They found that the second type correlated with happiness—the happiest students had roughly twice as many substantive talks as the unhappiest ones. Small talk, meanwhile, made up only 10 percent of their conversation, versus almost 30 percent of conversation among the least content students.

FAST-style question: Which replacement keeps the most precise academic meaning of “correlated”?

**A. Was associated with, because the researchers found a relationship between conversation type and happiness.**
B. Caused directly, because the excerpt proves every conversation produced happiness.
C. Was ignored by, because the researchers did not track conversation type.
D. Was confused with, because the researchers could not separate the two types.

Evidence points: cataloged | correlated | roughly twice as many

#### Card 2: Academic tone and precision
- Location: lines 33-40
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> In a series of experiments, psychologists gave Chicago commuters varying directions about whether to talk with fellow train passengers—something they typically avoided. Those told to chat with others reported a more pleasant journey than those told to “enjoy your solitude” or to do whatever they normally would. None of the chatters reported being rebuffed.

FAST-style question: Which word best preserves the academic tone of “reported” in this excerpt?

A. Bragged, because the sentence suggests the participants were showing off.
**B. Stated, because the sentence describes what participants communicated as a result.**
C. Whined, because the sentence suggests the participants were complaining.
D. Gossiped, because the sentence suggests the participants spread rumors.

Evidence points: experiments | reported | typically avoided | rebuffed

#### Card 3: Academic tone and precision
- Location: lines 41-46
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

> Small talk can also help us feel connected to our surroundings. People who smiled at, made eye contact with, and briefly spoke with their Starbucks baristas reported a greater sense of belonging than those who rushed through the transaction. Similarly, one not yet published paper found that when volunteers broke the silence of the Tate Modern to chat with gallerygoers, the visitors felt happier and more connected to the exhibit than those who were not approached.

FAST-style question: Which word best keeps the precise meaning of “transaction” in this context?

A. Argument, because the sentence describes people disagreeing with baristas.
B. Performance, because the sentence describes actors speaking to an audience.
**C. Exchange, because the sentence describes a brief interaction between customers and baristas.**
D. Experiment, because the sentence describes the result of one study.

Evidence points: sense of belonging | transaction | exhibit

Psychologists have long said that connecting with others is central to well-being, but just how much conversation we require is under investigation.

### ELA.9.R.1.1 — Key literary elements add layers of meaning

#### Card 1: Characterization adds meaning
- Location: paragraph 75
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> CAPULET. God’s bread, it makes me mad! Day, night, hour, ride, time, work, play, Alone, in company, still my care hath been To have her match’d, and having now provided A gentleman of noble parentage, Of fair demesnes, youthful, and nobly allied, Stuff’d, as they say, with honourable parts, Proportion’d as one’s thought would wish a man, And then to have a wretched puling fool, A whining mammet, in her fortune’s tender, To answer, ‘I’ll not wed, I cannot love, I am too young, I pray you pardon me.’ But, and you will not wed, I’ll pardon you. Graze where you will, you shall not house with me. Look to’t, think on’t, I do not use to jest. Thursday is near; lay hand on heart, advise. And you be mine, I’ll give you to my friend; And you be not, hang, beg, starve, die in the streets, For by my soul, I’ll ne’er acknowledge thee, Nor what is mine shall never do thee good. Trust to’t, bethink you, I’ll not be forsworn.

FAST-style question: What does Capulet’s response to Juliet reveal about him?

A. It reveals that he is patient because he calmly accepts Juliet’s refusal.
B. It shows that he gives Juliet complete freedom to choose what she wants.
**C. It reveals that he is controlling and angry because he threatens Juliet when she refuses to obey him.**
D. It proves that he is confused because he does not understand what Juliet said.

Evidence points: Thursday is near; lay hand on heart, advise. | God’s bread, it makes me mad | Graze where you will

> JULIET. Tell me not, Friar, that thou hear’st of this, Unless thou tell me how I may prevent it. God join’d my heart and Romeo’s, thou our hands; And ere this hand, by thee to Romeo’s seal’d, Shall be the label to another deed, Or my true heart with treacherous revolt Turn to another, this shall slay them both. Therefore, out of thy long-experienc’d time, Give me some present counsel, or behold ’Twixt my extremes and me this bloody knife Shall play the empire, arbitrating that Which the commission of thy years and art Could to no issue of true honour bring. > CHORUS. Two households, both alike in dignity, In fair Verona, where we lay our scene, From ancient grudge break to new mutiny, Where civil blood makes civil hands unclean. From forth the fatal loins of these two foes A pair of star-cross’d lovers take their life; Whose misadventur’d piteous overthrows Doth with their death bury their parents’ strife. The fearful passage of their death-mark’d love, And the continuance of their parents’ rage, Which, but their children’s end, nought could remove, Is now the two hours’ traffic of our stage; The which, if you with patient ears attend, What here shall miss, our toil shall strive to mend.

Evidence points: too rash, too unadvised, too sudden | Too like the lightning | cease to be

#### Card 3: Translate archaic or formal language
- Location: balcony excerpt
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> O Romeo, Romeo! wherefore art thou Romeo? Deny thy father and refuse thy name; Or, if thou wilt not, be but sworn my love, And I’ll no longer be a Capulet. ’Tis but thy name that is my enemy.

FAST-style question: What is the best plain-language paraphrase of Juliet’s words?

**A. Juliet wishes Romeo could reject his family name, or she could give up hers if he loves her.**
B. Juliet asks Romeo to introduce her to his father before they speak again.
C. Juliet says their family names make no difference and should be celebrated.
D. Juliet tells Romeo she will stop loving him unless he becomes a Capulet.

Evidence points: Deny thy father | refuse thy name | I’ll no longer be a Capulet

## The Lincoln-Douglas Debates — Stephen Douglas and Abraham Lincoln
- Source status: stored
- Local source available: yes
- Word count: 45,524
- Official mapped standards: ELA.9.R.2.4, ELA.9.R.3.2
- Standards with gold cards: ELA.9.R.2.4, ELA.9.R.3.2
- Standards still needing cards: none

### ELA.9.R.2.4 — Opposing arguments, claims, evidence, and validity

#### Card 1: Compare evidence development
- Location: paired debate excerpts: founders’ actions / limited claim
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Lincoln uses the founders’ actions as evidence: they restricted slavery in new territory and allowed the end of the African slave trade.
> Passage B: Lincoln then says he does not claim a right or desire to interfere with slavery where it already exists.

FAST-style question: How does Lincoln’s qualification in Passage B affect the support in Passage A?

A. It proves his evidence is false because he refuses to discuss the Constitution.
**B. It narrows the argument by showing his evidence supports restricting expansion, not interfering everywhere.**
C. It changes the topic from slavery to a personal complaint about public speaking.
D. It shows he supports expanding slavery into every new territory.

Evidence points: Historical examples support restricting slavery’s spread. | Lincoln limits the reach of the claim.

#### Card 2: Compare evidence development
- Location: paired debate excerpts: Missouri Compromise / natural rights
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Lincoln says repealing the Missouri Compromise is wrong because it lets slavery into Kansas and Nebraska and allows slavery to spread.
> Passage B: Lincoln clarifies that he does not argue for full social equality, but he defends natural rights: “life, liberty, and the pursuit of happiness.”

FAST-style question: Which statement best explains how the evidence develops Lincoln’s position?

**A. It shows he opposes slavery’s spread while defining the specific rights claim he is defending.**
B. It shows he supports slavery’s spread and rejects natural rights.
C. It proves his argument has no position on slavery or rights.
D. It shows he discusses only social equality and never mentions slavery.

Evidence points: Lincoln opposes slavery’s spread. | Lincoln defines the rights claim he is defending.

#### Card 3: Compare evidence development
- Location: paired debate excerpts: Nebraska Act / Dred Scott
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: The Nebraska Act claims local freedom over domestic institutions. A proposed amendment directly allowing voters to exclude slavery was voted down.
> Passage B: Lincoln points to Dred Scott as evidence that territorial voters cannot exclude slavery once it is brought there.

FAST-style question: How does Lincoln use evidence to challenge the claim of popular sovereignty?

**A. He shows that the law’s promise of local choice is weakened by a court decision limiting that choice.**
B. He shows that the Nebraska Act clearly gave territories unlimited power over slavery. The passage points to a different effect.
C. He argues that popular sovereignty has nothing to do with territorial government. This does not explain the author’s move.
D. He claims the amendment passed and fully protected the right to exclude slavery. The development of the passage points elsewhere.

Evidence points: The law claims local freedom. | The amendment vote and court decision limit that freedom.

#### Card 4: Evaluate argument effectiveness
- Location: paired debate excerpts: founders’ evidence / qualification
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Lincoln uses the founders’ actions as evidence: they restricted slavery in new territory and allowed the end of the African slave trade.
> Passage B: Lincoln then says he does not claim a right or desire to interfere with slavery where it already exists.

FAST-style question: Why does Lincoln’s combination of evidence and qualification make his argument more effective?

**A. It gives historical support while limiting the claim so opponents cannot easily misstate it.**
B. It avoids evidence and relies only on emotional language.
C. It makes the claim broader by demanding immediate interference everywhere.
D. It shifts away from slavery and discusses only personal reputation.

Evidence points: Historical support makes the argument grounded. | Qualification makes the claim harder to misrepresent.

#### Card 5: Evaluate argument effectiveness
- Location: paired debate excerpts: moral claim / rights limitation
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Lincoln calls indifference to slavery’s spread wrong because of “the monstrous injustice of slavery itself.”
> Passage B: Lincoln clarifies that he does not argue for political and social equality, but he does defend natural rights.

FAST-style question: Why might this argument be effective for Lincoln’s audience?

A. It avoids moral language and refuses to say what rights are involved.
B. It argues only for social equality and never mentions slavery.
C. It says slavery’s spread is unimportant because natural rights do not matter.
**D. It makes a moral claim against slavery while narrowing the issue to rights the audience recognizes.**

Evidence points: Lincoln uses moral language. | Lincoln narrows the issue to natural rights.

#### Card 6: Evaluate argument effectiveness
- Location: paired debate excerpts: popular sovereignty claim / legal rebuttal
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Popular sovereignty is presented as local freedom: people in a territory regulate slavery for themselves.
> Passage B: Lincoln answers with legal evidence from Dred Scott, arguing that territorial voters have no right to exclude slavery.

FAST-style question: Which argument is more effective for challenging popular sovereignty?

A. The popular sovereignty argument, because it proves courts cannot affect territorial voters. The passage points to a different effect.
B. Lincoln’s argument, because it avoids evidence and only repeats a slogan. This does not explain the author’s move.
**C. Lincoln’s argument, because it uses a specific legal decision to show that local choice may be limited.**
D. Neither argument is effective because both avoid the issue of territorial power. The development of the passage points elsewhere.

Evidence points: The first argument uses the appealing idea of local choice. | Lincoln’s argument uses a legal example to test whether local choice actually works.

#### Card 7: Evaluate claim validity
- Location: paired debate excerpts: equality exceptions / equality ideal
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Lincoln argues that narrowing “all men are created equal” would rub out the sentiment of liberty.
> Passage B: Lincoln compares equality to an ideal that people may not fully reach but still should use as a guide.

FAST-style question: Why is Lincoln’s defense of equality logically valid in these excerpts?

A. He proves equality is invalid because no person can ever act on an ideal. The passage points to a different effect.
B. He avoids the Declaration and supports his point only with unrelated insults. This does not explain the author’s move.
**C. He explains why weakening the principle is dangerous and why an imperfectly reached ideal can still guide action.**
D. He says exceptions to equality make liberty stronger for everyone. The development of the passage points elsewhere.

Evidence points: Lincoln explains the danger of exceptions. | Lincoln gives a reason an ideal can still guide action.

#### Card 8: Evaluate claim validity
- Location: paired debate excerpts: house-divided claim / Douglas criticism
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Lincoln says the country “cannot endure permanently half slave and half free” and predicts it will become “all one thing, or all the other.”
> Passage B: Douglas criticizes that claim as pushing the states toward forced uniformity and sectional conflict.

FAST-style question: Which evaluation best explains the validity of Douglas’s criticism?

**A. It is partly valid because it challenges the consequence of Lincoln’s claim, but it may overstate Lincoln’s position.**
B. It is fully valid because Lincoln directly calls for immediate war between North and South.
C. It is invalid because Douglas discusses a completely different topic from Lincoln.
D. It is fully valid because both speakers agree the nation can remain divided forever.

Evidence points: Lincoln makes a prediction about the nation’s future. | Douglas attacks the prediction by describing its possible consequences.

#### Card 9: Evaluate claim validity
- Location: paired debate excerpts: popular sovereignty / Dred Scott
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Popular sovereignty claims the people are free to regulate slavery in a territory.
> Passage B: Lincoln argues that Dred Scott leaves territorial voters with no right to exclude slavery once it is brought there.

FAST-style question: Which answer best evaluates the validity of the popular sovereignty claim?

**A. The claim is weakened because the evidence shows territorial voters may not actually control slavery.**
B. The claim is proven because both excerpts show voters can exclude slavery without limits.
C. The claim is unrelated because neither excerpt discusses territorial power.
D. The claim is strengthened because the amendment passed and expanded local choice.

Evidence points: The first claim promises local control. | The second excerpt supplies legal evidence against that control.

#### Card 10: Identify opposing claims
- Location: paired debate excerpts: Declaration equality / equality ideal
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Lincoln says Douglas’s reading of the Declaration narrows “all men are created equal” and risks rubbing out “the sentiment of liberty.”
> Passage B: Lincoln compares equality to a command to be perfect: even if people cannot fully reach it, the principle remains the standard that should guide action.

FAST-style question: What disagreement about equality is developed in these excerpts?

A. Lincoln argues that equality should be erased because no person can fully reach it.
B. Lincoln agrees that equality applies only to kings and not to ordinary people.
**C. Lincoln challenges a narrow view of equality and argues that equality should remain a guiding standard.**
D. Lincoln says the Declaration has no connection to arguments about slavery.

Evidence points: Lincoln challenges a narrow reading of equality. | Lincoln argues equality remains a guiding standard.

#### Card 11: Identify opposing claims
- Location: paired debate excerpts: house-divided claim / Douglas criticism
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Lincoln argues that the government “cannot endure permanently half slave and half free” and predicts it “will become all one thing, or all the other.”
> Passage B: Douglas criticizes Lincoln’s words as favoring “making all the States of this Union uniform” and “making war by the North upon the South for the extinction of slavery.”

FAST-style question: Which choice best compares Lincoln’s claim with Douglas’s criticism of that claim?

**A. Lincoln predicts the nation must resolve slavery, while Douglas frames that prediction as a threat of forced uniformity.**
B. Lincoln claims the issue is already solved, while Douglas agrees no conflict remains.
C. Lincoln argues for ignoring slavery, while Douglas says slavery should end immediately.
D. Both speakers claim that the nation can remain half slave and half free permanently.

Evidence points: Lincoln predicts the nation must resolve slavery. | Douglas frames that prediction as forced uniformity and conflict.

#### Card 12: Identify opposing claims
- Location: paired debate excerpts: Nebraska Act / Dred Scott response
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: The Nebraska Act says the people are “perfectly free to form and regulate their domestic institutions in their own way.”
> Passage B: Lincoln argues that Dred Scott says territorial voters have “no right to exclude slavery,” so if one person brings enslaved people into a territory, others have “no right to keep them out.”

FAST-style question: Which statement best identifies the opposing claims about popular sovereignty?

A. Both claims say territorial voters have complete power to exclude slavery. The passage points to a different effect.
**B. One claim says voters are free to decide slavery locally; Lincoln’s claim says that freedom is blocked by the court.**
C. Both claims say courts should not affect slavery in the territories. This does not explain the author’s move.
D. Lincoln’s claim says voters caused the court decision by rejecting slavery. The development of the passage points elsewhere.

Evidence points: One claim says voters are free to decide locally. | Lincoln claims that a court decision limits that local choice.

#### Card 13: Response to counterclaim
- Location: paired debate excerpts: Douglas criticism / Lincoln clarification
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Douglas’s criticism says Lincoln’s “house divided” claim means making all states uniform and making war by the North upon the South.
> Passage B: Lincoln answers by saying he did not call for interference where slavery already exists and that critics have made his claim too broad.

FAST-style question: How does Lincoln respond to Douglas’s criticism?

**A. He rejects the broad interpretation and clarifies that his claim is about slavery’s future direction.**
B. He accepts Douglas’s claim and calls for immediate war between regions.
C. He changes the subject by refusing to discuss slavery or the nation.
D. He says Douglas has no criticism and agrees with every part of the speech.

Evidence points: Douglas presents a criticism. | Lincoln responds by narrowing and clarifying his claim.

#### Card 14: Response to counterclaim
- Location: paired debate excerpts: local choice claim / Dred Scott rebuttal
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> Passage A: Popular sovereignty says people in a territory are free to regulate slavery for themselves.
> Passage B: Lincoln challenges that claim by pointing to Dred Scott, which he says leaves territorial voters with no right to exclude slavery.

FAST-style question: How does Lincoln’s reference to Dred Scott function as a response?

A. It supports popular sovereignty by proving voters have unlimited choice.
**B. It challenges the claim of local choice by showing a legal barrier to excluding slavery.**
C. It avoids the topic because court decisions cannot affect territories.
D. It answers the claim by saying slavery was already excluded everywhere.

Evidence points: Popular sovereignty is the claim. | Dred Scott is the counter-evidence.

### ELA.9.R.3.2 — Paraphrase grade-level text

#### Card 1: Chunk complex syntax
- Location: house divided excerpt
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> A house divided against itself cannot stand. I believe this government cannot endure, permanently half slave and half free. I do not expect the Union to be dissolved; I do not expect the house to fall; but I do expect it will cease to be divided.

FAST-style question: Which option best paraphrases Lincoln’s full point across these sentences?

A. Lincoln believes the nation will immediately break apart because no compromise is possible.
**B. Lincoln believes the nation will survive, but it cannot permanently remain split between slavery and freedom.**
C. Lincoln believes slavery and freedom can continue together without changing the country.
D. Lincoln believes the states should avoid discussing slavery to keep the Union peaceful.

Evidence points: cannot endure, permanently half slave and half free | I do not expect the Union to be dissolved | I do expect it will cease to be divided

#### Card 2: Paraphrase claim or theme
- Location: house divided excerpt
- Quality: Gold card
- Confidence: strong

Excerpt:

> A house divided against itself cannot stand. I believe this government cannot endure, permanently half slave and half free. I do not expect the Union to be dissolved; I do not expect the house to fall; but I do expect it will cease to be divided.

FAST-style question: Which statement best paraphrases Lincoln’s main point?

A. Lincoln argues that slavery and freedom can coexist permanently without conflict.
B. Lincoln argues that the government has already solved the slavery issue.
C. Lincoln argues that the Union must dissolve immediately.
**D. Lincoln argues that the United States cannot remain permanently divided over slavery.**

Evidence points: cannot endure | half slave and half free

## The Aeneid — Virgil
- Source status: stored
